2. **C) It was one of the first two stamps made in 1840** — The British Post Office produced the Penny Black and the Twopence Blue as the first stamps in 1840.

3. **A) They agreed to accept letters with prepaid stamps from other members** — After the UPS was formed, member countries agreed to accept letters carrying prepaid postage from other member countries.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — The speaker states that collecting stamps is one of the most popular hobbies in the world.

2. **Not Given** — The passage mentions that Rowland Hill was a British teacher who had the idea in 1840, but it does not state his age.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **took the letter to the person** — The sentence immediately defines 'delivered' with the clue 'or took the letter to the person'.

2. **paid for earlier by the sender** — The next sentence explains the word: 'That is, the sender paid for it earlier.'

3. **the first stamps** — 'They' refers back to 'the first stamps' mentioned in the previous sentence.

4. **the Universal Postal System (the UPS)** — 'This organization' refers to the Universal Postal System (the UPS), which was introduced earlier in the paragraph.

5. **True** — The first paragraph explains that the sender paid in their own country and the person in the other country paid for the rest of the trip.

6. **False** — Rowland Hill, a British teacher, had the idea of the postage stamp, whereas a German organized the Universal Postal System later in 1874.

7. **No Information** — The passage says other countries started making stamps quickly, but it does not mention which country was second.

8. **False** — The passage states that today the offices of the UPS are only in Switzerland.

9. **Some countries did not want to accept letters with the stamps of other countries.** — This sentence in paragraph 3 directly states that some countries refused letters carrying foreign stamps.

10. **Collecting stamps is one of the most popular hobbies in the world, and every stamp collector knows about the Penny Black and the Twopence Blue.** — This final sentence states that stamp collecting is a very popular worldwide hobby.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **C) The invention and international development of the postage stamp** — The passage traces the invention of the postage stamp in Britain, the creation of the UPS for international mail, and the popularity of stamp collecting today.

2. **B) Receivers did not have to pay to get their letters** — Because the sender bought the stamp and placed it on the letter, the receiver did not have to pay for the letter upon delivery as people used to do before 1840.

3. **D) Agree to receive and handle** — In the third paragraph, 'accept' refers to countries agreeing to take and deliver letters bearing stamps from other countries.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summary: The Invention of the Postage Stamp):**

Before 1840, sending a letter abroad was difficult because both the sender and the receiver had to pay separately. Then Rowland Hill, a British teacher, invented the postage stamp, and Great Britain made the first prepaid stamps in 1840. Later, in 1874, the Universal Postal System was created so countries could accept each other's stamps. Today, almost every country belongs to this organization, and stamp collecting is a popular hobby.


**Task 2 (Opinion Paragraph: Letters in the Digital Age):**

In the past, sending letters by post was the main way for people in different cities or countries to communicate. Today, however, most people prefer emails and instant messages because they are fast and free. Still, sending a real letter or postcard can be very special. A handwritten letter feels personal and shows that the sender took time to write. Moreover, people often save letters and stamps as memories. Therefore, even in the digital age, traditional mail still has an important place.


---


## Passage 68: Volcanoes (`raw1_068` · `A2`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **Sicily**

2. **magma**

3. **cone**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) It comes from the island of Vulcano, once thought to be Vulcan's home** — In classical times, people believed the island of Vulcano was the home of Vulcan, the god of destruction.

2. **A) Hot lava, pieces of rock, and large amounts of gas** — The speaker mentions that three materials come out of a volcano's vent: hot liquid lava, pieces of rock, and great quantities of gas.

3. **D) By the type of vent and kind of explosion** — Scientists classify volcanoes according to the type of vent and the kind of explosion.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **False** — Although science has improved greatly, the speaker says we still know very little about volcanoes.

2. **True** — The speaker states that volcanoes have destroyed cities, forests, and farmland throughout history.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **the god of destruction** — The passage defines Vulcan directly with '- the god of destruction'.

2. **a kind of chimney (an opening in a volcano)** — The text introduces 'vent' as an alternative word for 'a kind of chimney'.

3. **a liquid deep in the earth** — The passage directly defines 'magma' as 'a liquid deep in the earth'.

4. **a hot liquid that comes out of a volcano's vent** — The passage introduces '(lava)' right after 'a hot liquid' among the materials coming out of the vent.

5. **group** — The passage gives the definition directly with 'i.e. group'.

6. **people** — 'Them' refers back to 'people' who are frightened by earthquakes and volcanoes.

7. **volcanoes** — 'They' refers to 'volcanoes' in the first clause of the sentence.

8. **the island of Vulcano** — 'It' refers to 'the island of Vulcano' mentioned in the previous sentence.

9. **True** — The text states: 'Science has improved greatly. However, we still know very little about volcanoes.'

10. **False** — The three materials that come out of the vent are lava, pieces of rock, and gas; magma is deep inside the earth.

11. **False** — The passage states that lava and rock (not gas) collect around the vent to form the cone.

12. **True** — The final sentence states that scientists classify volcanoes according to the type of vent and kind of explosion.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **B) Volcanoes are both attractive and dangerous natural features** — The author contrasts people's fear with pleasure and notes that volcanoes are beautiful mountains even though they destroy cities, forests, and farmland.

2. **C) Magma is deep inside the earth, whereas lava comes out of the vent** — Magma is deep inside the earth, while lava is the hot liquid that comes out of the vent onto the surface.

3. **A) amounts** — In paragraph 2, 'great quantities of gas' means large amounts of gas.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summary: How Volcanoes Work):**

Volcanoes are both beautiful and dangerous mountains that have caused great destruction throughout history. A volcano works like a chimney, or vent, reaching down to hot liquid magma deep inside the earth. When a volcano erupts, lava, rock, and large amounts of gas come out of the vent. Lava and rock build up around the opening to create a cone, and scientists group volcanoes by their vent and explosion type.


**Task 2 (Opinion Paragraph: Living Near a Volcano):**

Living near a volcano can be both exciting and risky. On the one hand, volcanic mountains are often very beautiful, and the soil around them is usually rich for farming. Many tourists also visit volcanoes, which helps local businesses. On the other hand, volcanoes are dangerous because an eruption can destroy homes, forests, and farmland at any time. Even with modern science, we still do not know everything about volcanoes. For this reason, living near an active volcano requires careful safety plans.


---


## Passage 69: Containers (`raw1_069` · `A2`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **metal**

2. **wide mouth**

3. **flexible**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) Because they are usually made of heavy paper** — The speaker says that cartons are usually made of cardboard (heavy paper) and are not very durable.

2. **B) Liquid foods like soup and various chemicals** — Beverages and liquid foods like soup, as well as photographic, industrial, and medical chemicals, are kept in bottles.

3. **A) By folding, tying, or knotting them** — The speaker mentions folding, tying, and, for plastic bags, knotting as ways to close bags.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — The speaker says jars are very practical because we can clean them and use them again.

2. **False** — The speaker clearly states that the article does not talk about containers used in cooking and serving food.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **some of the simple and basic containers** — 'Them' refers back to 'some of the simple and basic containers' in the preceding sentence.

2. **a can opener** — 'This utensil' refers to 'a can opener' mentioned right before the semicolon.

3. **other jars** — In 'Some jars have tops or covers called screw-on-lids and others have lids that fit inside the mouth', 'others' refers to other jars.

4. **Bottles** — 'They' refers to 'Bottles' from the previous sentence.

5. **bags which are made of paper, foil, or thin plastic** — 'Such bags' refers to the bags made of paper, foil, or thin plastic described in the previous sentence.

6. **strong and long-lasting** — The next sentence defines 'durable' directly: 'In other words, they are strong and long-lasting.'

7. **heavy paper** — The passage puts the definition in parentheses right after the word: 'cardboard (heavy paper)'.

8. **c) you can take it off the box** — The text explains 'removable tops' in parentheses: '(i.e., you can take these tops off)'.

9. **top or cover (for a jar)** — The sentence calls screw-on-lids 'tops or covers' that some jars have.

10. **liquid food** — The text introduces soup with the phrase 'and other liquid food such as soup'.

11. **soft and movable** — The next sentence explains the word: 'That is, it is soft and movable.'

12. **Paint cans, beverage cans, and spray cans (any two).** — The Cans section specifically names paint cans, beverage cans, and spray cans.

13. **Because they are made of glass or ceramic.** — The passage states that a jar is a glass or ceramic container, which makes it durable but breakable by accident.

14. **b) five basic containers** — The article introduces and describes five basic types of containers: cans, boxes and cartons, jars, bottles, and bags.

15. **d) glass or plastic** — The Bottles section states that a bottle is usually made of glass, and plastic bottles are also very common.

16. **c) tops** — Each section describes the different lids, caps, or tops used to open and close boxes, jars, and bottles.

17. **b) the size of the mouth** — The passage says: 'a bottle has a small mouth and a neck, but a jar has a wide mouth and no neck.'


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **C) They last a long time if handled carefully, but can shatter easily** — The author explains that while jars are strong enough to be reused, they can still shatter easily if dropped by accident.

2. **B) Thin plastic bag** — The passage contrasts cans (strong and long-lasting) with cartons and thin bags, which are not very durable and are often thrown away.

3. **D) To make readers aware of how important containers are in daily life** — The questions at the end invite readers to notice how many different containers they depend on every day and at work.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summary: Five Basic Containers):**

The passage describes five basic containers: cans, boxes and cartons, jars, bottles, and bags. Metal cans are cylindrical and durable, while boxes and cartons have square or rectangular sides with hinged or removable tops. Glass or ceramic jars have a wide mouth and can be reused, though they are breakable. Bottles, made of glass or plastic, have a small mouth and a neck. Finally, bags are flexible containers usually thrown away after use.


**Task 2 (Opinion Paragraph: Reusable vs. Disposable Containers):**

In everyday life, single-use plastic bags and bottles create a lot of waste. In my opinion, we should replace disposable containers with reusable ones whenever possible. For example, glass jars can be washed and used again to store food at home, and cloth bags are strong enough for weekly shopping. Although plastic bags and cardboard cartons are cheap and convenient, using durable containers helps protect the environment and keeps our cities cleaner.


---


## Passage 70: Luminous and Non-Luminous Bodies (`raw1_070` · `A2`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **reflects**

2. **129 degrees Celsius / 129°C**

3. **translucent substances / translucent**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) It does not produce light and only reflects sunlight.** — The narrator states that the Moon is non-luminous because it does not produce its own light and only sends the Sun's light back to Earth.

2. **B) They let light pass through so we can see clearly through them.** — According to the passage, transparent substances let light pass through so we can see what is on the other side, which is why windows are made of clear glass.

3. **A) Neither light nor sight can pass through them.** — The passage explains that light cannot pass through opaque substances, and we cannot see anything behind them.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — The passage gives an electric lamp and a fire, along with the Sun, as examples of luminous bodies that emit light.

2. **False** — The passage states that iron and glass are non-luminous bodies, not luminous ones.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **True** — The passage states that most bodies which emit light also emit heat, and gives the Sun, an electric lamp, and a fire as luminous bodies.

2. **True** — The passage says the Sun emits light and heat, adds 'So does an electric lamp or a fire,' and calls them luminous bodies.

3. **False** — The Sun emits light; it is the Moon that reflects the light from the Sun.

4. **False** — The passage states that the Moon does not produce light and we see it only because it reflects light from the Sun.

5. **True** — When there is no sunlight on the Moon, it is very cold, with temperatures reaching 129°C below zero.

6. **True** — Opaque substances do not let light pass through and prevent us from seeing anything behind them, unlike transparent substances.

7. **send out** — The passage provides the definition immediately after the word: 'emit, or send out'.

8. **a) the Sun, an electric lamp, a fire** — In the preceding sentences, the passage introduces the Sun, an electric lamp, and a fire as bodies that emit light.

9. **b) the Moon** — The clause explains why the Moon is not a luminous body: 'it' refers back to 'The Moon'.

10. **a) substances** — The previous sentence introduces 'some substances' that are not clear, so 'them' refers to those substances.

11. **c) They don't produce heat or light.** — Using the Moon as an example, the passage states that non-luminous bodies neither produce light nor produce heat.

12. **a) They are clear substances.** — The passage defines transparent substances as clear substances through which light can pass so we can see what is on the other side.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **B) How light is produced and how it interacts with different materials** — The passage first explains the difference between luminous and non-luminous bodies, then classifies materials as transparent, translucent, or opaque based on how light passes through them.

2. **C) It is a translucent substance.** — Frosted bathroom glass lets light enter while hiding the shapes of objects behind it, matching the passage's definition of translucent substances.

3. **D) send out** — In the sentence 'We can see these bodies because they give out light,' 'give out' is used interchangeably with 'emit, or send out'.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Guided Summary: Light and Substances):**

Luminous bodies, such as the Sun, a fire, and an electric lamp, produce their own light and usually heat. Non-luminous bodies, like the Moon, iron, and glass, do not produce light or heat; we see the Moon only because it reflects sunlight. Materials also affect light differently. Transparent substances like clear glass let us see objects behind them, translucent ones let only light pass, and opaque materials block light completely.


**Task 2 (Everyday Uses of Light Materials):**

In our daily lives, we depend on both transparent and opaque materials for comfort and safety. In a typical home, transparent glass is essential for windows because it lets natural sunlight enter while allowing us to see outside. However, we cannot use clear glass everywhere. Opaque materials like brick walls and wooden doors are necessary for privacy, warmth, and protection from bad weather. In addition, translucent glass is very useful in bathrooms because it brings in daylight without letting anyone see inside. Choosing the right material makes a house practical and comfortable.


---


## Passage 71: Coal (`raw1_071` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **smelting copper**

2. **Anthracite / anthracite**

3. **400 years / four hundred years**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **B) It lies closer to the surface and is more abundant than petroleum and natural gas.** — The speaker mentions two advantages: coal is found closer to the surface (so it is cheaper to mine), and there is more coal than petroleum or natural gas.

2. **C) It burns slowly and keeps a steady, uniform fire.** — According to the passage, anthracite burns slowly, maintains a uniform and constant fire, and gives the most heat, making it ideal for domestic use.

3. **A) Coal, petroleum, and natural gas are equally important today.** — The speaker states that coal was the leading fuel toward the end of the 19th century, whereas today coal, petroleum, and natural gas are of equal importance.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — The passage states that people used coal the most after the invention of the steam engine during the 18th and 19th centuries.

2. **False** — The passage notes that one disadvantage of anthracite is that there is less of it than of the other kinds of coal.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **coal** — The clause defines 'mine coal' as taking 'it' (coal) from under the earth.

2. **petroleum and natural gas** — Earlier in the paragraph, the passage compares coal with petroleum and natural gas, calling them 'the other two fuels'.

3. **anthracite** — The sentence states that anthracite's properties make 'it' (anthracite) the most preferable kind of coal.

4. **lignite, subbituminous coal, and bituminous coal** — Four kinds of coal were introduced earlier; excluding anthracite leaves lignite, subbituminous coal, and bituminous coal.

5. **to take coal from under the earth** — The text gives the definition right after the semicolon: 'that is, take it from under the earth'.

6. **to give out** — The passage places the meaning in parentheses immediately after the verb: 'produces (gives out)'.

7. **to keep** — The passage defines 'maintains' using dashes right after the word: 'maintains - keeps -'.

8. **for uses in our homes** — The passage explains the phrase immediately afterward: 'in other words, for uses in our homes'.

9. **True** — The first sentence states that coal is taken from below the surface of the earth.

10. **False** — The passage states that the Chinese used coal at least 3,000 years ago, not less than 3,000 years ago.

11. **True** — The passage states: 'In Europe, they started using coal in the 12th century in England.'

12. **False** — The passage explicitly states that today, coal, petroleum, and natural gas are fuels of equal importance.

13. **False** — Anthracite has the highest carbon content (86% or more) and gives the most heat, whereas lignite has less carbon (about 75%) and gives little heat.

14. **True** — The text says subbituminous coal contains less moisture than lignite, which implies lignite contains moisture.

15. **invention of the steam engine** — The passage states: 'But people used coal the most after the invention of the steam engine, during the 18th and 19th centuries.'

16. **fuel** — The passage groups coal, petroleum, and natural gas together as fuels of equal importance.

17. **lignite; bituminous coal and anthracite** — The four types are ordered from lowest to highest heat/carbon: lignite, subbituminous coal, bituminous coal, and anthracite.

18. **burns slowly; maintains (keeps) a uniform and constant fire** — The passage states that anthracite burns slowly and thus maintains (keeps) a uniform and constant fire, making it preferable for domestic uses.

19. **produce steam** — The third paragraph notes that coal is used 'to produce steam in steam engines'.

20. **about 400 years** — The final sentence states: 'We'll probably have enough of it for about 400 years.'

21. **Because coal is found closer to the surface of the earth than petroleum and natural gas.** — The passage states that coal lies closer to the surface than petroleum and natural gas, allowing us to mine it less expensively.

22. **There is less of it than there is of the other kinds of coal.** — The passage explicitly states: 'One disadvantage of anthracite, however, is that there is less of it than there is of the other kinds of coal.'


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **C) Coal with higher carbon content generally produces more heat.** — In the second paragraph, lignite has 75% carbon and gives little heat, while anthracite has 86% or more carbon and gives the most heat, showing a direct link between carbon content and heat output.

2. **A) To classify the four types of coal and compare their properties** — The passage contrasts the four types of coal by carbon percentage, heat output, moisture, burning speed, and availability, giving a comprehensive overview of coal types.

3. **B) even and steady** — In 'maintains - keeps - a uniform and constant fire,' 'uniform' describes a fire that stays even and steady rather than changing unpredictably.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summary: Coal and Its Varieties):**

Coal has been used as a fuel for thousands of years, becoming especially important after the steam engine was invented. Compared with petroleum and natural gas, coal is cheaper to mine because it lies closer to the surface and is more abundant. There are four main types of coal: lignite, subbituminous coal, bituminous coal, and anthracite. Anthracite has the highest carbon content, burns slowly, and produces the greatest, steadiest heat, making it ideal for home heating despite its limited supply. Today, coal is used in homes, metal smelting, and steam engines, with reserves lasting roughly 400 years.


**Task 2 (The Future of Coal as an Energy Source):**

Although coal reserves are large enough to last for centuries, modern societies should gradually reduce their dependence on coal and invest in cleaner energy sources. On one hand, coal remains an attractive fuel because it is abundant, located close to the earth's surface, and relatively inexpensive to mine. For developing economies or heavy industries such as iron smelting, coal provides reliable and affordable energy that supports industrial growth. On the other hand, burning coal releases high levels of carbon and pollutants into the atmosphere, contributing to air pollution and climate change. Moreover, high-quality coal like anthracite is already scarce compared to lower grades. Therefore, while coal cannot be abandoned overnight, governments should use existing energy resources responsibly while expanding solar, wind, and other renewable technologies. A balanced transition will protect both economic stability and the environment for future generations.


---


## Passage 72: Cars of the Future (`raw1_072` · `A2`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **air pollution / pollution**

2. **intelligent roads**

3. **fewer accidents**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) To protect the environment and stop air pollution** — The passage states that the most important cause leading to a change in car design will be environmental, so cars will run on electricity to avoid air pollution.

2. **B) Enter the car and tell the computer their destination** — According to the narrator, a passenger will simply get into the car and say where they want to go, and the computer will do the rest.

3. **A) From special power strips built into the road** — The passage explains that special intelligent roads will contain strips supplying electrical power to the vehicles as they drive along them.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — The passage states that tomorrow's models will not be able to move on ordinary roads and will require special intelligent roads.

2. **False** — The passage says the next twenty years will bring greater change in car models than the past fifty years, and tomorrow's cars will not look like today's.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **cars** — 'Ones' replaces the plural noun 'cars' mentioned in the opening question.

2. **tomorrow's cars** — 'They' refers back to 'tomorrow's cars' in the preceding sentence.

3. **special intelligent roads** — The previous sentence introduces 'special intelligent roads', which 'These roads' refers back to.

4. **the vehicles** — The clause describes the vehicles moving along the roads: 'they' refers to 'the vehicles'.

5. **True** — The passage explicitly states that tomorrow's models will not be able to move on ordinary roads and will require special intelligent roads.

6. **False** — The passage says the next twenty years will bring greater change in car models than the past fifty years, and tomorrow's cars will not look like those of today.

7. **False** — The passage identifies three problems with today's cars: pollution, heavy traffic ('the first of the last two'), and traffic accidents.

8. **c) run on electricity** — The passage states that tomorrow's cars will be electrically powered and run on electricity entirely.

9. **d) do the driver's job** — Computers will drive the cars instead of human drivers and take passengers wherever they want to go.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **B) Air pollution, heavy traffic, and traffic accidents** — The passage mentions three main problems with today's cars: air pollution, heavy traffic, and traffic accidents, and describes electric and computer-driven cars as solutions.

2. **D) Around twenty years before 2015** — Since the passage mentions 'the year 2015' and 'the next twenty years', the text was written around 1995 and describes a vision of the future from that time.

3. **A) prevented** — In the sentence 'The last two may also be avoided if computers drive the cars,' 'avoided' means prevented from happening.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Guided Summary: Future Cars):**

In the future, cars will look very different because they will be built to stop air pollution. They will run entirely on electricity and stay clean for the environment. Computers will drive the cars instead of people, so passengers will only need to say their destination. To make this possible, special intelligent roads will be built to supply electrical power during trips. As a result, there will be less traffic and far fewer road accidents.


**Task 2 (Traveling in a Self-Driving Car):**

Driving a self-driving electric car would be both exciting and convenient. On the positive side, I would feel happy knowing that my car does not pollute the air. Also, I could relax, read a book, or talk with friends during long trips because the computer would handle all the driving safely. However, I might feel a little nervous at first trusting a machine on busy streets. Building special roads everywhere could also take a long time and cost a lot of money. Overall, I think self-driving electric cars will make travel safer and cleaner.


---


## Passage 73: Using Electricity (`raw1_073` · `A2`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **toasters**

2. **electricity**

3. **accurate / correct**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) Washing machines** — The passage lists washing machines, refrigerators, and mixers as examples of appliances that use shaft power to run electric motors.

2. **B) Medical treatments** — The passage states that special lamps produce ultraviolet rays and infrared rays, and both are used in medical treatments.

3. **D) They allow doctors to see inside the human body.** — An X-ray lets doctors see inside the human body so they can make a more accurate diagnosis of an illness.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **False** — The passage explicitly states that we do not use electricity in our homes only; we also need it in schools, hospitals, banks, factories, and other places.

2. **Not Given** — The passage mentions that X-rays help doctors diagnose illnesses, but it does not state whether X-rays are expensive.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **electricity** — The previous sentence states that we don't use electricity in our homes only, so 'it' refers back to electricity.

2. **shaft power (power that we use to run electric motors)** — The first sentence of the second paragraph defines shaft power as power used to run electric motors, which is 'this kind of power'.

3. **ultraviolet rays and infrared rays** — The preceding sentence introduces lamps that produce ultraviolet rays and infrared rays.

4. **a doctor (a physician)** — In "when a doctor looks at an X-ray, he can tell you what kind of an illness you have," 'he' refers back to 'a doctor'.

5. **machines (Clue: "- machines -" between dashes)** — The passage immediately defines 'devices' in apposition between dashes: '- machines -'.

6. **doctors (Clue: ", or doctors,")** — The passage defines 'physicians' directly using the phrase ', or doctors,'.

7. **correct (Clue: "; that is, correct,")** — The passage explains 'accurate' immediately afterward with '; that is, correct,'.

8. **Electric fires, irons, and toasters (any two).** — Paragraph 1 states: "at home we use it for heating electric fires, irons and toasters."

9. **Subway trains, trolley buses, and electric locomotives (any two).** — Paragraph 2 states that vehicles such as subway trains, trolley buses and electric locomotives have motors that run on electricity (shaft power).

10. **the inside of the human body** — Paragraph 3 explains: "When we look at an X-ray, we can see the inside of the human body, and in this way the physicians, or doctors, can give a more accurate... diagnosis of an illness."

11. **Electric fires, irons (or toasters, washing machines, refrigerators, mixers - any two)** — Paragraphs 1 and 2 list electric fires, irons, toasters, washing machines, refrigerators, and mixers as devices used at home.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **B) Various ways electricity is used in daily life, transport, and medicine** — The passage covers everyday uses of electricity at home and in public places, shaft power for appliances and vehicles, and medical applications such as ultraviolet/infrared rays and X-rays.

2. **A) Identification of the nature of an illness** — In the passage, 'diagnosis' is paired with 'tell you what kind of an illness you have' after looking at an X-ray, so it means identifying a disease or illness.

3. **C) Both household appliances and certain vehicles rely on electric motors.** — The passage explains that shaft power runs electric motors in appliances (washing machines, refrigerators, mixers) and vehicles (subway trains, trolley buses, electric locomotives), whereas irons and toasters are examples of heating.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Guided Summary: Uses of Electricity):**

Electricity is essential in many places, including homes, schools, hospitals, and factories. At home, people use it for heating appliances like irons and toasters, as well as for running electric motors in washing machines and refrigerators. Electricity also powers vehicles such as subway trains. In medicine, it is used to produce ultraviolet rays, infrared rays, and X-rays, which help doctors see inside the body and diagnose illnesses accurately.


**Task 2 (Opinion Paragraph: The Most Important Use of Electricity):**

In my opinion, the medical uses of electricity are the most important for human life. While heating devices and washing machines make daily life comfortable, X-rays and special medical lamps directly save lives. Because of X-rays, doctors can look inside the human body and find illnesses quickly and accurately. Without electricity in hospitals, physicians could not diagnose or treat many serious health problems effectively. Therefore, electricity plays a vital role in keeping people healthy.


---


## Passage 74: Polymer Batteries for Electric Vehicles (`raw1_074` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **air pollution / pollution**

2. **5 kilograms / five kilograms**

3. **toxic**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **B) They are too heavy, too expensive, and lack enough power.** — The passage explains that lead-acid batteries do not provide enough power for a long distance, must be replaced in time, and are too heavy and expensive.

2. **C) It can be formed into any shape and placed anywhere in the car.** — Because car builders can form the conductive polymer battery into any shape, it can be placed under the roof, behind door panels, or under the seat.

3. **A) The polymers used to make it are inexpensive.** — The passage states that the polymer battery is cheap because of the polymers used.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — The passage explicitly mentions that there are already trucks, vans, and other vehicles which run on electricity.

2. **False** — The passage states that the conductive polymer battery is made of a material similar to plastic, even though it has a metallic appearance.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **a kind of vehicle (which runs on electricity)** — The sentence reads "Although there are trucks, vans and other vehicles which run on electricity," showing that a van is a type of vehicle.

2. **even kill people (be poisonous/deadly)** — The passage explains 'toxic' directly after a dash: "the polymer battery can be toxic - it can even kill people".

3. **fuel** — In "there is only a certain amount of fuel in the world and it will eventually finish," 'it' refers back to 'fuel'.

4. **another reason (for developing electric cars)** — The paragraph begins with "There is an interest in developing electric cars for two reasons. One reason is... Another is...", so 'Another' refers to another reason.

5. **the conductive polymer battery (the new type of battery)** — The previous sentences introduce the conductive polymer battery, so 'it' refers to that battery.

6. **that car builders can form the battery into any shape** — In "In addition, car builders can form it into any shape. This means you can place the battery anywhere in the car", 'This' refers to the ability to form the battery into any shape.

7. **vehicles (the fuel-powered vehicles which we use today)** — In "They believe that such vehicles will replace the ones which we use today," 'ones' refers back to 'vehicles'.

8. **c) electric passenger cars are not common today** — The passage states that because of lead-acid batteries, the auto industry cannot yet produce electric passenger cars to sell on the market.

9. **a) It is made of a kind of conductive plastic.** — The passage describes it as a conductive polymer battery made of a material similar to plastic that has the ability to conduct electricity.

10. **1) They do not provide enough power for a long distance, 2) They must be replaced in time, 3) They are too heavy and too expensive.** — Paragraphs 2 and 3 state that lead-acid batteries do not provide the necessary power for long distances, must be replaced in time, and are too heavy and too expensive.

11. **1) It is three times stronger than an ordinary car battery and weighs less than 5 kilograms, 2) It can be formed into any shape and placed anywhere in the car, 3) It is cheap because of the polymers used.** — Paragraph 3 highlights its strength and light weight, flexibility in shape/placement, and low cost.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **C) Why electric passenger cars have been delayed and how polymer batteries may solve the problem** — The passage explains why electric cars are needed, why lead-acid batteries limit passenger cars, and how conductive polymer batteries offer a promising solution despite one safety drawback.

2. **D) Covered with protective material to prevent danger** — In the passage, 'insulated well' is needed so the toxic polymer battery does not harm or kill people, meaning it must be covered or protected safely.

3. **A) Conductive polymer batteries are not yet widely used in commercial passenger cars.** — Although several types of polymer batteries are ready to be tested, the passage treats them as a 'first important step' toward mass production, implying electric passenger cars are still in development.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summary & Paraphrase: From Lead-Acid to Polymer Batteries):**

There is growing interest in electric cars because global fuel supplies are limited and electric vehicles do not pollute the air. Currently, lead-acid batteries prevent mass production of electric passenger cars because they are heavy, costly, wear out over time, and lack power for long trips. Scientists have addressed these issues by developing conductive polymer batteries, which are lightweight, inexpensive, much stronger, and flexible enough to fit anywhere in a car. However, because they can be toxic, they require careful insulation before widespread use.


**Task 2 (Opinion Mini-Essay: The Future of Electric Vehicles):**

Governments should actively support the development and adoption of electric vehicles, provided that safety concerns are carefully managed. First, fossil fuels will eventually run out, so societies must transition to cleaner transportation before an energy crisis occurs. Second, reducing air pollution in cities directly improves public health. Although early battery technologies have faced obstacles such as high costs, limited driving range, and potential toxicity, continued research and strict safety regulations can overcome these challenges. Investing in electric vehicles today is therefore a necessary step toward a cleaner and more sustainable future.


---


## Passage 75: Effects of Cigarette Smoking (`raw1_075` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **taste**

2. **oxygen-starved**

3. **nicotine**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **A) Lung cancer** — The passage states that cigarette smokers who inhale the smoke can develop lung cancer, while those who only take smoke into their mouths can suffer from throat, tongue, and larynx cancers.

2. **C) It lowers oxygen levels in the blood and brain and can harm others too.** — The passage explains that increased carbon monoxide intake reduces oxygen intake, lowering oxygen levels in the blood and brain, and can cause permanent damage even to non-smokers nearby.

3. **D) Satisfying their dependence creates a psychological feeling of relief.** — According to the passage, feeling calm is only a psychological consequence of satisfying a need; physiologically, cigarettes are arousing agents that make people more nervous.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **False** — The passage explicitly states that nothing can completely eliminate the tar without completely changing the taste of the cigarette.

2. **Not Given** — The passage compares cigarettes to coffee and tea as arousing agents, but it does not state whether coffee and tea contain carbon monoxide or cause permanent brain damage.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **breathe it in** — The passage defines 'inhale' immediately afterward: "who inhale the smoke, or breathe it in".

2. **remove it (completely)** — The passage explains 'eliminate' with ", i.e., remove,".

3. **lasts for a very long time** — The passage defines 'permanent damage' as "damage which lasts for a very long time".

4. **have enough oxygen (get enough oxygen)** — The passage explains 'An oxygen-starved brain' in parentheses as "(a brain without enough oxygen)".

5. **need it / cannot do without it** — The passage states that smokers need cigarettes because of their dependence on nicotine: "In other words, they can't do without it."

6. **strong and fast-acting stimulant** — The passage describes nicotine directly as "the strong and fast-acting stimulant called nicotine".

7. **cigarette smokers** — In "It is true that cigarette smokers need cigarettes. Of course they do not actively choose to harm themselves and others", 'they' refers back to 'cigarette smokers'.

8. **cigarettes (arousing agents)** — In "because cigarettes, like coffee and tea, are arousing agents and they make people more nervous, not less", 'they' refers to cigarettes (the arousing agents).

9. **Smokers who only take the smoke into their mouths (without inhaling it).** — Paragraph 1 states: "Other smokers who only take the smoke into their mouths can suffer from throat, tongue and larynx cancers."

10. **Developing low-tar brands of cigarettes and using certain forms of filters in addition to the cigarette's own filter.** — Paragraph 1 explains that low-tar brands and extra filters can help reduce the tar, though nothing removes it completely without changing the taste.

11. **Because cigarettes are arousing agents (like coffee and tea) that contain nicotine, a strong stimulant, so they actually stimulate the nervous system rather than calm it.** — Paragraph 3 states that smoking cannot physiologically soothe nerves; instead, cigarettes are arousing agents that make people more nervous.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **B) The specific dangers of tar, carbon monoxide, and nicotine in cigarettes** — Each paragraph focuses on one of the three harmful substances in cigarettes—tar, carbon monoxide, and nicotine—and explains its specific effects on the body and mind.

2. **C) Stimulating or increasing nervous activity** — The passage contrasts 'arousing agents' with calming or soothing nerves, explaining that they 'make people more nervous, not less', so 'arousing' means stimulating or exciting.

3. **A) People near smokers can suffer long-term harm from carbon monoxide.** — Paragraph 2 states that carbon monoxide can cause permanent damage 'on others besides the smokers themselves', implying secondhand exposure harms non-smokers nearby.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summary: The Three Harmful Substances in Cigarettes):**

Cigarettes contain three dangerous substances: tar, carbon monoxide, and nicotine. Tar causes various cancers, and while low-tar brands and extra filters reduce the risk, tar cannot be fully removed without ruining the taste. Carbon monoxide reduces oxygen in the blood and brain, causing permanent harm to both smokers and people nearby. Finally, nicotine creates strong dependence. Although smokers believe cigarettes relax them, smoking actually stimulates the nervous system and increases nervousness.


**Task 2 (Opinion Mini-Essay: Banning Smoking in Public Places):**

Smoking in enclosed public places should be strictly banned because it harms not only smokers but also innocent people nearby. As the passage explains, carbon monoxide from cigarettes reduces oxygen supply to the brain and can cause permanent damage to others besides the smokers themselves. Non-smokers should not have to breathe toxic smoke in restaurants, workplaces, or public transport. Although smokers may struggle with nicotine dependence, protecting public health is more important than personal convenience. A ban also encourages smokers to reduce their consumption and seek help to quit.


---


## Passage 76: Microscopic Organisms (`raw1_076` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **animalcules**

2. **chlorophyll**

3. **electron microscope**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) It has a cell membrane and a cell wall, but no nucleus.** — The passage states that bacteria have only one cell, which lacks a nucleus and contains only a cell membrane and a cell wall.

2. **B) Because they are inactive outside a cell and reproduce only inside one** — The passage explains that outside another cell a virus is inactive and only reproduces after entering another cell, making it hard to classify as living or non-living.

3. **D) Protists and monera** — At the end of the passage, the speaker explains that some bacteriologists added two more kingdoms: the protists and the monera.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — The passage states that both animal-like and plant-like protists provide food for other plants and animals that live in the water.

2. **False** — The passage explicitly states that a virus is not a cell and is simpler than a cell, lacking cytoplasm and a nucleus.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **a) It is difficult to classify microscopic organisms.** — Throughout the passage, the author focuses on the characteristics of protists, bacteria, and viruses that make them hard to classify into the plant or animal kingdoms.

2. **a, c, f** — The passage states that microscopic organisms differ from plants and animals (a), some are not complete cells such as bacteria lacking a nucleus and viruses lacking cytoplasm (c), and viruses are difficult to classify as living or non-living (f).

3. **the studies of microscopic organisms (bacteria and protozoa)** — The sentence directly defines Bacteriology and Protozoology as "the studies of microscopic organisms."

4. **having only one cell** — The following sentence explains: "They have only one cell."

5. **to make more of itself / make more copies** — The next sentence clarifies: "It makes more viruses."

6. **not active; unable to act or reproduce** — The following sentence contrasts it: "It does not become active and reproduce until it enters another cell."

7. **these organisms (microscopic organisms / bacteria and protozoa)** — "them" refers back to "these organisms" in the same sentence.

8. **scientists** — "they" refers to the subject "Scientists" at the beginning of the sentence.

9. **some protists (the animal-like protists)** — "They" refers to "Some protists" mentioned in the preceding sentence ("Some protists are like animals.").

10. **protists** — The paragraph contrasts single-celled protists with other ("Others") multicelled protists.

11. **the cell (of a bacterium)** — "It" refers to "the cell" in the previous sentence ("They have only one cell, but the cell does not have a nucleus.").

12. **Four kingdoms: the plant kingdom, the animal kingdom, the protist kingdom (protists), and the monera kingdom.** — The passage refers to the plant and animal kingdoms as well as two additional kingdoms: the protists and the monera.

13. **Plant-like protists have chlorophyll and can make their own food, whereas animal-like protists do not have chlorophyll and get their food from other organisms.** — Paragraphs 2 and 3 contrast the two groups based on chlorophyll and how they obtain food.

14. **Both animal-like and plant-like protists provide food for other plants and animals that live in the water (and neither belongs to the plant or animal kingdom).** — Paragraph 3 explicitly states: "Both animal and plant-like protists provide food for other plants and animals that live in the water."

15. **a) Bacteria are single-celled organisms with a cell membrane and cell wall, whereas a virus is not a cell and has no cytoplasm or nucleus (only a protein cover and reproductive material). b) Bacteria are much larger than viruses (which can only be seen with an electron microscope), and bacteria can exist actively outside another cell while viruses are inactive until they enter a cell.** — Paragraphs 4, 5, and 6 detail differences in cellular structure, size/visibility, and activity/reproduction between bacteria and viruses.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **C) Viruses depend completely on host cells in order to multiply.** — Paragraph 6 describes how a virus enters a cell, multiplies until the cell breaks open, and moves into other cells, showing its dependence on host cells to replicate.

2. **B) To explain why microscopic organisms are hard to group into existing kingdoms** — The author explains that while we cannot see microscopic organisms, they are everywhere and play an important role in life on Earth, while posing classification challenges.

3. **A) They lack a nucleus and obtain food from other organisms.** — Paragraph 4 states that bacteria have only one cell, lack a nucleus, and cannot make their own food, so they must get food from other organisms.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summary: Classifying Microscopic Organisms):**

Microscopic organisms are difficult to group into the plant or animal kingdoms because they share mixed or incomplete cellular features. For instance, some protists resemble animals and consume other organisms, whereas others contain chlorophyll like plants and make their own food. Bacteria consist of a single cell without a nucleus, so some scientists place them in the monera kingdom. Viruses are even simpler, lacking both cytoplasm and a nucleus, and they remain inactive until they enter and reproduce inside another cell. Because of these differences, scientists added the protist and monera kingdoms.


**Task 2 (Opinion Paragraph: Why Microbiology Matters in Daily Life):**

Learning about microscopic organisms is essential for everyday life, even though we cannot see them with the naked eye. First, these tiny organisms play a major role in nature and human health. For example, plant-like protists produce food in water ecosystems, while certain bacteria and viruses cause diseases that doctors and scientists must understand to treat effectively. Second, knowing how these organisms function helps us make better daily choices regarding hygiene, food safety, and medicine. When people understand that viruses need living cells to multiply or that bacteria live everywhere around us, they are more likely to wash their hands and use antibiotics responsibly. Therefore, basic knowledge of microbiology benefits everyone, not just scientists.


---


## Passage 77: Roller-Skating Takes Off (`raw1_077` · `A2`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **seven / 7**

2. **polyurethane**

3. **40,000 / forty thousand**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) Because the new skates with polyurethane wheels are expensive** — The passage mentions that in New York's Central Park people wait in line to rent skates on weekends, and many prefer to rent because the new skates are expensive.

2. **A) A two-hour skating trip through Central Park on Tuesday nights** — The passage states that on Tuesday nights, city skaters join in "Nightskates", a two-hour trip through Central Park.

3. **D) The Olympic Games** — Skaters hope that roller-skating will become part of the Olympic Games in the near future.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — The passage specifically mentions that famous singer Linda Ronstadt once skated to a dinner party in Los Angeles.

2. **Not Given** — The passage states that the new skates are expensive, but it does not mention how much it costs to rent them in Central Park.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **He puts on a suit and tie every weekday morning and roller-skates seven miles across San Francisco to his office.** — The first sentence describes John Buchan's unusual morning commute by roller-skates in business clothes.

2. **a) They make skating very easy. b) They are expensive.** — Paragraph 2 states that polyurethane wheels make skating very easy, while noting that the new skates are expensive.

3. **False** — The passage says "Nightskates" is a two-hour trip through New York's Central Park, not through New York streets.

4. **False** — The passage says that in addition to ("As well as") providing transportation and fun, roller-skating is also becoming an organized sport—not that those are the reasons people call it a sport.

5. **True** — The passage states that national teams will compete in next year's American Games and skaters hope it will become part of the Olympic Games in the near future, meaning it has not yet become an international Olympic/Games sport.

6. **d) roller-skating is becoming popular** — The idiom "takes off" means to suddenly become very popular or successful, matching the passage's description of the new interest in roller-skating.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **B) Roller-skating is growing quickly in popularity for work, fun, and sport.** — The passage shows John Buchan skating to work, Linda Ronstadt skating to a party, people lining up in Central Park, and skaters organizing into national teams, demonstrating widespread popularity.

2. **C) New skates with polyurethane wheels that make skating easy** — The second paragraph directly explains that the new interest in roller-skating is the result of a new kind of skate with polyurethane wheels that make skating very easy.

3. **A) It is used both as a practical way to travel and as a social hobby.** — Because John Buchan skates seven miles to work and skaters join Nightskates in Central Park, it can be inferred that people see roller-skating as both practical and enjoyable.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Guided Summary: The Rise of Roller-Skating):**

Roller-skating is becoming very popular in American cities. People use roller skates to go to work, attend parties, or have fun in parks like Central Park. This growing interest comes from new skates with polyurethane wheels, which make skating much easier. Although the new skates are expensive, many people rent them. Finally, roller-skating is also turning into a serious sport with national teams and thousands of members.


**Task 2 (Opinion Paragraph: Roller-Skating as Transportation):**

I think roller-skating is a great way to travel in a city, especially for short trips. First, it is much faster than walking and does not produce any air pollution like cars or buses. In addition, skating to school or work is good exercise and helps people stay healthy without spending extra time at a gym. Of course, busy streets can be dangerous, so skaters should wear helmets and use bike lanes or parks when possible. Overall, if a city has safe roads, roller-skating can be both fun and practical.


---


## Passage 78: Learning About Mars (`raw1_078` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **moons**

2. **Olympus Mons**

3. **krypton**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **A) 24 hours, 37 minutes, and 27 seconds** — The passage states that Mars takes 24 hours, 37 minutes, and 27 seconds to complete one turn around itself.

2. **C) Twenty-two** — In 1965, Mariner 4 flew past Mars and sent Earth twenty-two photographs of its surface.

3. **B) They were able to repair the damage themselves** — According to the text, Viking 1 and Viking 2 could self-repair, meaning they could repair damage to a part of the spacecraft themselves.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **False** — The passage explicitly states that Mariner 4 flew past Mars in 1965 and did not land on it.

2. **Not Given** — The passage mentions when Viking 2 landed and notes that both spacecraft sent new information, but it gives no details about their weight or scientific instruments.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **Earth and Mars** — The opening sentence compares Earth and Mars ('Earth and Mars have many characteristics in common'), so 'both planets' refers to Earth and Mars.

2. **a) using the English word canals for the Italian word canali** — The text states that 'canali' properly means 'channels', but entered English as 'canals' (man-made waterways), leading people to believe living things built them.

3. **c) on Earth** — Writing from Earth, Dr. Grady compares the four volcanoes discovered on Mars by Mariner 9 with the volcanoes here on Earth.

4. **very small** — In the text, the two objects around Mars are described as 'these two very small objects', so 'tiny' means very small.

5. **b) they could repair the damage themselves** — The following sentence explicitly defines 'self-repair': 'when there was a problem with a part of the spacecraft, they could repair the damage themselves.'

6. **i) Olympus Mons** — The passage notes that Mariner 9 discovered four volcanoes on Mars and names the largest one as Olympus Mons.

7. **k) strong winds and rocky surface** — The text states it was very difficult for Viking 1 to land safely 'because of the strong winds and the rough rocky surface of Mars.'

8. **g) lakes, seas, forests and narrow lines** — The passage states that Schiaparelli's 1877 map 'showed lakes, seas and forests. It also showed narrow lines.'

9. **m) radio signals from Earth** — The passage states: 'Radio signals from Earth controlled the two spacecraft.'

10. **b) August 20, 1975 - July 20, 1976** — Viking 1 left Cape Canaveral on August 20, 1975, and landed safely on Mars on July 20, 1976.

11. **e) krypton and xenon** — The Vikings discovered krypton and xenon in the Martian atmosphere, whereas the other gases listed were already known.

12. **d) twenty-four hours, thirty-seven minutes, twenty-seven seconds** — Paragraph 1 states: 'It takes Mars twenty-four hours, thirty-seven minutes and twenty-seven seconds to complete one turn.'

13. **True** — The passage states that the Martian day is 24 hours, 37 minutes, and 27 seconds, so it is a bit longer than Earth's day; therefore, Earth's day is shorter.

14. **False** — The text explicitly says of Schiaparelli's map: 'Although this was not the first map of Mars, it created a lot of interest among astronomers.'

15. **True** — Viking 1 took eleven months to travel to Mars, which is almost one year.

16. **False** — Dr. Grady names four spacecraft (Mariner 4, Mariner 9, Viking 1, and Viking 2), but explicitly notes Mariner 4 did not land on Mars and only states that Viking 1 and Viking 2 landed there.

17. **True** — The final paragraph states that astronomers 'expect to find some form of life there in the future.'


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **C) The Italian word 'canali' was mistranslated into English as 'canals'** — Schiaparelli used the Italian word 'canali' (correctly translated as 'channels'), which entered English as 'canals' (man-made waterways), causing people to assume intelligent beings built them.

2. **A) Uneven and rocky** — The passage says strong winds and a 'rough rocky surface' made it very difficult for Viking 1 to find a safe landing spot, indicating an uneven, rocky terrain rather than a smooth or soft one.

3. **D) He doubts scientists can be completely certain about Martian life without going there** — Dr. Grady concludes with 'In my opinion, they won't know that for sure until they go to Mars,' implying that robots and distant observations cannot provide final certainty without human travel.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summarizing Early Mars Exploration):**

In the nineteenth century, similarities between Earth and Mars led astronomers to believe life existed there, especially after Schiaparelli's 1877 map and the mistranslation of 'canali' as man-made canals. Space missions later revealed a different reality. In 1965, Mariner 4 sent back photographs showing craters and volcanic rock, and in 1971, Mariner 9 discovered huge volcanoes like Olympus Mons. Finally, in 1976, Viking 1 and Viking 2 landed on Mars, controlled by radio signals, and discovered new gases such as krypton and xenon in its atmosphere.


**Task 2 (Human vs. Robotic Exploration of Mars):**

At the end of the passage, Dr. Grady suggests that humans must travel to Mars to be certain about life there. Although sending astronauts is exciting, I believe robotic spacecraft are currently a better choice than human missions. First, sending people to Mars is extremely expensive and dangerous because of the long journey, strong winds, and rough surface. Second, modern robots and landers can already explore difficult areas, take photographs, and analyze gases in the atmosphere without risking human lives. As technology improves, automated machines can even repair themselves and collect samples safely. In the future, if spacecraft become faster and safer, astronauts may visit Mars. Until then, robotic exploration gives us valuable scientific knowledge at a much lower cost and risk.


---


## Passage 79: Pirates (`raw1_079` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **200 / two hundred**

2. **rags**

3. **privateer**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) They elected their own captains and created rules.** — The speaker explains that pirate groups practiced a kind of democracy by electing their own captains and preparing rules and regulations for their business.

2. **B) Books and movies created a romantic picture of them as handsome gentlemen.** — The passage states that books like "Treasure Island" and "Captain Blood," along with movies, helped create a romantic image of pirates.

3. **A) Anne Bonney and Mary Read** — The passage lists Anne Bonney and Mary Read as famous women pirates at the end of the talk.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **False** — The passage explicitly states that most pirates did not live long.

2. **True** — The passage states that Spanish treasure ships carrying gold and silver from Spanish colonies to Europe were frequently attacked by pirates during the 1600s and 1700s.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **200** — Paragraph 2 states that pirates existed for about 200 years, from the 16th to the 18th century.

2. **Treasure Island; Captain Blood** — Paragraph 3 mentions "Treasure Island" by Robert Louis Stevenson and "Captain Blood" by Rafael Sabatini.

3. **gold earrings and a large hat or turban; a pistol or gun** — Paragraph 3 describes the movie/book pirate as wearing gold earrings and a large hat or turban, with a pistol or gun in his hand.

4. **along the American coasts** — Paragraph 6 states that there was a lot of piracy along the American coasts and in the West Indies during the 1600's and 1700's.

5. **Jean Lafitte** — Paragraph 6 cites Jean Lafitte as a pirate who became a privateer and helped protect New Orleans in the War of 1812.

6. **famous woman pirate** — Paragraph 7 lists Anne Bonney and Mary Read among the famous women pirates in history.

7. **b) is taken by using force** — The previous clause explains that pirates used to "take people to their ships by using force" and demand ransom to free a captured person.

8. **a) money** — Ransom is described as a large amount that was paid to free a captured person.

9. **c) which are close to the sea** — Pirates left their ships to break into homes in coastal towns, meaning towns located along the coast near the sea.

10. **b) ugly clothes** — The sentence states that pirates "dressed very badly in rags", showing that rags refers to worn, ugly clothes.

11. **a) fought against** — The passage pairs "rebelled" directly with "and fought against the government".

12. **a) try to escape from the police** — The text defines outlaws as "people who committed crimes and hid from the authorities", while the police looked for them everywhere.

13. **a) carry valuable goods** — The sentence explains that Spain's treasure ships carried gold and silver from Spanish colonies to Europe.

14. **True** — Paragraph 1 lists "picaroons" as one of the other names for pirates.

15. **True** — Paragraph 1 states that pirates were sea robbers or bandits.

16. **False** — The text states that pirates rebelled against the government and lived as outlaws, rather than being well-paid by the government.

17. **False** — Paragraph 3 says the "good-looking gentleman" is the romantic image of pirates in films and books, not real pirates.

18. **True** — Paragraph 7 names both famous male pirates and famous women pirates (Anne Bonney and Mary Read).

19. **False** — Paragraph 4 explicitly states: "In reality, pirates were not romantic at all."

20. **False** — Pirates were sea robbers and outlaws; Jean Lafitte later became a privateer who helped American military forces, rather than pirates being a military force themselves.

21. **True** — Paragraph 4 states that pirates were often "desperate, violent and dangerous people".

22. **True** — Paragraph 6 explains that many American pirates became privateers during the American War for Independence and the War of 1812.

23. **True** — Paragraph 4 mentions that pirates "wasted food and money".


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **B) To contrast the popular romantic image of pirates with historical reality** — Paragraphs 3 and 4 contrast the romanticized image of pirates from books and movies with the harsh reality of their violent, desperate lives, while also covering their history and organization.

2. **C) Former pirates who fought on behalf of a government during wartime** — The text says Jean Lafitte and other American pirates became privateers when the American government asked for their help in wartime, implying privateers operated with government approval against enemy forces.

3. **D) Many people became pirates due to dissatisfaction with their own society.** — The passage mentions that pirates rebelled because they did not like the situation in their home society, and that they drank a lot, dressed in rags, and were hunted as outlaws, showing they lived unstable lives outside normal society.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Fiction vs. Reality: The Life of Pirates):**

Books and movies often portray pirates as handsome, romantic gentlemen wearing gold earrings and carrying swords or pistols. However, the passage explains that real pirates were desperate, violent, and dangerous outlaws who dressed in rags and died young. Despite their criminal lifestyle, pirate crews practiced a form of democracy by electing their captains and making rules. Furthermore, during wars, governments sometimes hired pirates like Jean Lafitte as privateers to help defend their coasts.


**Task 2 (Why Piracy Still Fascinates People):**

In my opinion, stories about pirates remain popular mainly because they symbolize adventure, freedom, and rebellion. Modern audiences enjoy escaping from ordinary rules through exciting tales set on the open ocean, such as "Treasure Island." However, I believe it is important not to forget the harsh truth behind these legends. Real pirates attacked innocent towns, stole cargo, and lived dangerous, short lives as outlaws. While films focus on treasure maps and brave captains, history reminds us that piracy involved violence and crime rather than glamour. Appreciating the difference between entertaining fiction and historical reality helps us enjoy these stories without ignoring the suffering that real piracy caused.


---


## Passage 80: Geothermal Energy (`raw1_080` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **degrees Celsius / degrees**

2. **generators**

3. **cold water**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) 25 degrees Celsius per kilometre** — The passage states that a normal heat gradient is 25 degrees Celsius per kilometre, whereas 40 degrees Celsius per kilometre is a very good heat gradient.

2. **B) Hot water and steam already come out of the ground naturally.** — In California, New Zealand, Italy, and Iceland, hot water and steam already come out of the ground, so people do not have to drill there.

3. **D) To heat a thousand houses for thirty or forty years** — The speaker explains that the hot water found in Southampton is enough to heat a thousand houses for thirty or forty years.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — The passage states that the hot water and steam in Iceland are hot enough to heat most of Reykjavik, the capital.

2. **False** — The passage explicitly states that geothermal energy will never be an important source of energy overall, even though it is very useful in many places.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **b) California, New Zealand, Italy and Iceland** — The preceding sentence names California (USA), New Zealand, Italy, and Iceland as the most famous examples where hot water and steam come out of the ground.

2. **cold water** — Scientists send cold water down to the bottom, and "it" (the cold water) gets hot there.

3. **geothermal energy** — The previous sentence states that "Geothermal energy will never be an important source of energy," and "it" refers back to geothermal energy.

4. **c) made holes in the ground** — Earlier in paragraph 1, the passage explains that you "make a deep hole in the ground" to find hot water, and then gives drilling in Southampton as an example.

5. **provide the power to work** — The passage defines 'drive' directly in the phrase "i.e. provide the power to work".

6. **False** — Paragraph 1 states that 25°C per kilometre is a normal heat gradient, whereas 40°C per kilometre is a very good heat gradient.

7. **False** — Paragraph 4 states that geothermal energy will never be an important source of energy, though it can be useful in many places.

8. **In some places you do not have to drill to obtain geothermal energy.** — This is the opening sentence of paragraph 2 and directly expresses the same meaning.

9. **b) want to use the heat of the underground hot dry rock** — Paragraph 3 explains that scientists in Los Alamos are drilling down to hot dry rock and circulating water to bring its heat to the surface.

10. **a) They make electricity from geothermal energy in California.** — Paragraph 2 states that hot water and steam are hot enough to drive electric generators in California. The other options contradict the passage (engineers did find hot water in England; hot water is not always present; scientists send cold water down in Los Alamos).


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **A) By drilling two holes, breaking the rock, and circulating water through it** — Paragraph 3 explains step-by-step how scientists in Los Alamos plan to drill down, break the hot dry rock, pump cold water down, and bring heated water back up through another hole.

2. **C) Objective and realistic, noting both its practical uses and its limits** — The passage presents geothermal energy neutrally and realistically: it describes real-world uses in England, Iceland, and California, ongoing research in Los Alamos, and concludes that while it will never be a major global source, it can still be very useful locally.

3. **B) get** — In the sentence "In some places you do not have to drill to obtain geothermal energy", the context shows people are trying to get or acquire energy from underground.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (How We Obtain Geothermal Energy):**

Geothermal energy comes from the heat beneath the Earth's surface, and it can be collected in three ways. First, engineers can drill deep holes to reach underground hot water, as they did in Southampton, England, to heat houses. Second, in regions like Iceland and California, natural hot water and steam already reach the surface, providing power for heating and electric generators. Finally, where only hot dry rock exists, scientists in Los Alamos are drilling down to break the rock and pump cold water through it so that heated water returns to the surface.


**Task 2 (Investing in Geothermal Energy):**

Although geothermal energy cannot replace all fossil fuels worldwide, I believe countries with high underground heat should invest more in it. First, geothermal heat is a clean and reliable resource that does not depend on sunny or windy weather. As the passage shows, places like Reykjavik already heat most of their homes using natural hot water and steam, which reduces the need for imported oil or coal. Even in areas without natural steam, projects like the one in Los Alamos demonstrate how new technology can unlock heat from dry underground rock. While drilling can be expensive at first, the long-term benefits of lower emissions and steady local heating make geothermal energy a smart investment for suitable regions.


---


## Passage 81: Bali (`raw1_081` · `A2`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **boats**

2. **1950s / 1950's**

3. **food**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) People spend more time making things for tourists than for their temples.** — The passage explains that the Balinese produce more and more items for tourists and fewer for their temples, leaving less time for their gods and fewer festivals.

2. **B) To make sure Bali changes for the better, not for the worse.** — The authorities are trying to organise and control tourism carefully so that Bali changes for the better rather than for the worse.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **False** — The passage states that almost everybody on the island is an artist of some kind, not just a small group of people.

2. **True** — The passage states that tourists bring both money and new ideas, and that the Balinese need both.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **Bali** — 'There' refers back to 'Bali' in the opening sentence.

2. **the Balinese people** — 'Them' refers back to 'the Balinese people' in the previous sentence.

3. **tourists** — 'They' refers to 'more tourists' in the preceding sentence.

4. **the Balinese** — 'They' refers back to 'The Balinese' at the beginning of the previous sentence.

5. **Bali** — 'It' refers to 'Bali' at the beginning of the same sentence.

6. **d) complete** — 'The whole world' means their complete or entire world; Bali was everything to them.

7. **a) make** — 'Produce' here means to make or create goods (art and crafts) for tourists.

8. **b) other countries** — Buying things 'from abroad' means importing them from other countries.

9. **In the evenings, they make music, dance, paint or carve things out of wood and stone.** — Paragraph 1 states that after working all day, they spend their evenings doing these artistic activities.

10. **They began to worry about the outside world in the 1950's (when tourists began to visit Bali).** — Paragraph 2 says that until the 1950's they did not worry about the outside world, and then tourists began to visit.

11. **Because they produce more and more things for tourists and make fewer things for their temples.** — Paragraph 2 directly links making goods for tourists to having less time for their gods and fewer festivals.

12. **They need the money and new ideas that tourists bring, especially because too many people live on the island now and they need to buy food and other things from abroad.** — Paragraph 3 explains that tourists bring needed money and ideas, and the large population requires buying goods from abroad.

13. **There will be less money on the island.** — Paragraph 3 notes that if fewer tourists come to the island, there will be less money.

14. **Because if there are too many tourists, the Balinese way of life will change too quickly.** — Paragraph 3 explains that too many tourists would cause the local way of life to change too fast.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **C) To show both the benefits and the risks of tourism in Bali and the need for careful control** — The passage describes both the economic benefits of tourism (money, buying food from abroad, new ideas) and the drawbacks (fewer temple festivals, rapid change to traditional life), showing why authorities want balanced control.

2. **B) Tourism has created an economic dependence that competes with traditional religious life.** — The Balinese are shifting their artistic work from temples to tourists, resulting in fewer festivals, yet they still need tourist money to support a growing population.

3. **A) in a positive way** — In the last sentence, 'for the better, not for the worse' contrasts positive, beneficial change with harmful change.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Guided Summary: Tourism in Bali):**

Bali is a beautiful island where almost everyone is an artist. Before the 1950s, the Balinese lived a traditional life focused on their temples and festivals. When tourists started arriving, tourism became a big business. Today, people make more art for visitors, so they have less time for religious festivals. Although the island needs tourist money and new ideas, the authorities carefully control tourism to protect the local way of life.


**Task 2 (Opinion Paragraph: Benefits and Risks of Tourism):**

Tourism brings both benefits and problems to popular places. On the positive side, visitors spend money, create jobs, and help local people buy things they need. Tourists also bring new ideas and help people learn about different cultures. However, too much tourism can harm local traditions. When people spend all their time working for tourists, they may forget their own festivals and customs. Therefore, governments should control the number of visitors so that tourism helps the community without destroying its culture.


---


## Passage 82: Life in Space (`raw1_082` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **half**

2. **300,000 / 300000**

3. **radio waves**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **B) By measuring small variations in a star's light caused by orbiting planets.** — The passage explains that Dr. Campbell and his colleagues used high-resolution spectroscopy to measure small variations in a star's light caused by unseen planets pulling the star out of its path.

2. **C) 10,000 million** — Of the 100,000 million stars in our galaxy, about one fifth (20,000 million) are stable like our sun, and half of those may have planets, leaving 10,000 million stars that might have life.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **False** — The passage states that planets do not shine on their own; they only reflect light from stars.

2. **Not Given** — The passage mentions that SETI will study radio waves from stars, but it does not state when or where SETI was founded.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **Canadian astronomers** — 'Their' refers back to 'Canadian astronomers' in the previous sentence.

2. **nearby stars** — 'Them' refers to 'nearby stars' earlier in the same sentence.

3. **these unseen objects** — 'They' refers back to 'these unseen objects' in the preceding sentence.

4. **a new astronomical measuring technique** — Paragraph 4 directly defines 'high-resolution spectroscopy' as 'a new astronomical measuring technique'.

5. **massive (or large / huge)** — In the passage, 'tiny' (very small) is contrasted with stars that are 'massive or larger'.

6. **larger (very large / heavy)** — The passage explicitly glosses 'massive' as 'or larger'.

7. **give back** — The passage gives the definition in parentheses right after the word: 'reflect (give back)'.

8. **ways** — The passage defines 'paths' immediately afterward with 'in other words, their ways'.

9. **unchanging (and cool)** — The passage defines 'stable' immediately afterward as 'that is, unchanging and cool like our own sun'.

10. **About 100,000 million (100 billion).** — Professor Roy states there are '100,000 million stars' in our galaxy.
