11. **Stars are bigger than planets.** — Paragraph 5 states that planets are tiny objects compared with stars, and the sun is 300,000 times more massive than Earth.

12. **Epsilon Eridani and Gamma Cephei.** — Paragraph 7 names Epsilon Eridani and Gamma Cephei as the two stars definitely affected by large orbiting bodies.

13. **The Space Telescope and a European satellite called Hipparchus.** — Paragraph 10 mentions these two upcoming space projects.

14. **planets and stars** — Paragraph 5 contrasts planets and stars in two ways: size (tiny vs. massive) and light (reflecting light vs. shining).

15. **"There are about 100,000 million stars in our galaxy and about one fifth are stable, that is, unchanging and cool like our own sun," said Professor Roy.** — One fifth of 100,000 million equals 20,000 million stars that are unchanging and cool like our sun.

16. **F (Fact)** — This is a factual finding observed through high-resolution spectroscopy measurements.

17. **O (Opinion)** — The words 'many scientists believe' and 'we may have' indicate a prediction or belief rather than a verified fact.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **C) To report new astronomical evidence suggesting that planets and life may be common in our galaxy** — The passage focuses on how Canadian astronomers' new observations using high-resolution spectroscopy increase hopes of finding planets and potentially life around other stars.

2. **A) extremely small** — The passage describes future projects that will detect 'minute variations in stars' motions', meaning tiny or very small movements.

3. **D) Roughly half of the 16 observed stars showed signs of orbiting companions.** — Of 16 nearby stars studied, two definitely had orbiting bodies and five or six others appeared to have unseen companions, totaling seven or eight out of 16 (roughly half).


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summary: Searching for Other Worlds):**

Canadian astronomers have strengthened hopes of finding life beyond Earth by discovering that nearly half of nearby stars may have orbiting planets. Previously, finding planets was difficult because they are much smaller than stars and only reflect light. However, researchers solved this problem by using high-resolution spectroscopy to detect small changes in starlight caused by the gravitational pull of unseen planets. Because roughly one fifth of our galaxy's stars are stable like the sun, scientists estimate that 10,000 million stars could support life. Upcoming projects like the Space Telescope, Hipparchus, and SETI may soon help answer whether we are alone.


**Task 2 (Opinion Mini-Essay: Is the Search for Extraterrestrial Life Worthwhile?):**

For centuries, humans have wondered whether life exists beyond Earth. In my opinion, searching for extraterrestrial life is a worthwhile investment, even though it requires advanced technology and significant funding.

First, studying other planetary systems helps us understand how our own solar system formed and what conditions make a planet suitable for life. Projects such as space telescopes and radio wave monitoring also drive technological innovation, which often leads to practical tools we use on Earth, from better sensors to satellite communications.

On the other hand, critics argue that money spent on space research should go toward solving urgent problems like poverty or climate change. While those issues certainly deserve priority, scientific exploration does not have to stop completely. Discovering whether life exists elsewhere would be one of the greatest breakthroughs in human history.

In conclusion, balanced funding for space research expands human knowledge and inspires future generations while still allowing society to address everyday challenges.


---


## Passage 83: Nice Not Easy (`raw1_083` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **six / 6**

2. **patisserie**

3. **language school**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) She had to do all the housework in a large villa instead of just light chores.** — Alison says her contract stipulated caring for the children and light housework, but in fact she had to do all the housework in the large villa.

2. **B) Because the agent earned a fee for every new girl introduced.** — The agent in Nice believed the employer rather than Alison because the agency collected a fee for every new girl they introduced.

3. **A) It can help au pairs meet other people through a language school.** — Alison notes at the end that using an agency is worthwhile if only to meet other au pairs through a language school, and adds that boys have also been known to work as au pairs.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **False** — Alison says she was usually working from 7 a.m. until 9 p.m. with a two-hour break after lunch, and she was not even allowed to eat without Madame H.'s permission.

2. **Not Given** — The passage mentions that Alison found her second family through a friend and lived above their patisserie, but it does not say how much money they paid her.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **d) girls** — "Most" refers back to the "thousands of girls" who go abroad each year to become au pairs.

2. **b) the agent in Nice** — The sentence explains that the agent in Nice believed the employer instead of Alison because the agent (referred to as "they") received a fee for every new girl introduced.

3. **a) To improve language skills; b) to discover other cultures; c) to find some independence.** — The opening paragraph mentions these three goals as the most common reasons young people become au pairs abroad.

4. **a) She did not let Alison eat without her permission; b) She did not let Alison have a key to the house (also acceptable: she did not let her drink their biological milk or stay out past 10 p.m. on her evening off).** — Paragraph 5 describes how Madame H. controlled Alison's food, refused her a key, and set a strict 10 p.m. curfew.

5. **False** — Although her contract promised light housework, Alison actually had to do all the housework in the large villa.

6. **False** — She worked twelve hours a day (7 a.m. to 9 p.m. minus a two-hour break), but for six days a week, not seven.

7. **True** — After six weeks with the first family, she announced she was leaving and later stayed with a second family for three months.

8. **False** — She spent three very happy months with her second family, living above their patisserie.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **C) She was completely inexperienced and naive.** — Saying she was "as green as the England I left behind" is an idiom meaning she was very naive and inexperienced when she arrived in Nice.

2. **B) Her daily working conditions were far stricter and harder than her contract promised.** — The contract promised "une vie de famille" (family life) and light housework, yet she had to do all the housework, ask permission to eat, and obey strict curfews without a key.

3. **D) Talking with other au pairs helped Alison realize her treatment was unacceptable.** — Alison states that she talked to other girls and became convinced her treatment was unfair, and she also mentions meeting other au pairs at a language school as a benefit of agencies.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summary of Alison's Experience):**

Alison became an au pair in Nice to gain experience abroad, but her first placement was much harder than her contract promised. Although she was supposed to care for three children and do light housework, she had to clean the entire villa and work twelve hours a day, six days a week, for £140 a month. Her employer also restricted her food, refused her a key, and gave her almost no freedom. After six weeks, she left and later enjoyed three happy months with a kind family above their patisserie.


**Task 2 (Pros and Cons of Working as an Au Pair):**

Working as an au pair abroad can be a rewarding experience, provided that young people prepare carefully beforehand. Living with a host family offers a great opportunity to practice a foreign language every day and learn about another culture from the inside. It also helps young adults become more independent. However, Alison's story shows that problems can arise when contracts are unclear or employers take advantage of inexperienced workers. To stay safe, future au pairs should ask for detailed written agreements about working hours and duties, and connect with other au pairs or language schools for support. With proper preparation, working abroad can be both safe and unforgettable.

## Passage 84: The Olympic Marathon (`raw1_084` · `A2`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **490 BC**

2. **300 / three hundred**

3. **1928**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **B) Once every four years in Olympia** — The passage explains that the Greeks held the ancient Olympic festival once every four years in Olympia and even stopped their wars for the games.

2. **C) To remember a Greek soldier's run from Marathon to Athens** — In 1896, the organizers included the marathon so that the Greek soldier's long run from Marathon to Athens would not be forgotten.

3. **A) Governments pay for athletes' expenses because they want them to win.** — Today, governments pay for their athletes' training, travel, and pocket-money because they want them to win, leading some people to call the games a political marathon.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — The passage states that the games were so important to the ancient Greeks that they even stopped their wars for them.

2. **False** — According to the passage, Olympic athletes never earn as much money as professional sportsmen and are often students or teachers of a sport.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **the marathon** — "It" refers to "the marathon" mentioned at the end of the previous sentence.

2. **Olympia** — "There" refers back to Olympia, where the Greeks held the festival every four years.

3. **the games (the Olympic Games)** — "Them" refers to "these games" (the ancient Olympic Games) in the previous sentence.

4. **Athens** — "There" refers to Athens, where the modern Olympic Games were started again in 1896.

5. **1896 (when the modern Olympic Games were started again)** — "Since then" means since 1896, the year the modern Olympics restarted in Athens.

6. **Olympic athletes** — "They" refers to "Olympic athletes" from the preceding sentence.

7. **c) place** — The passage states that the name 'marathon' comes from a village (a place) in Greece.

8. **b) the distance between Marathon and Athens was run by a Greek soldier** — The organizers included the marathon in 1896 so that the Greek soldier's run from Marathon to Athens would not be forgotten.

9. **b) the governments spend money on their athletes** — Because governments pay for their athletes' training, travel, and pocket-money to help them win, some people feel the games have become a political marathon.

10. **That the Greeks had won the war in Marathon.** — The passage explains that the soldier ran from Marathon to Athens when the Greeks won the war in 490 BC.

11. **Since 1896 (when the first modern Olympic Games were held).** — The organisers of the first modern Olympic Games in 1896 included the marathon, and it has been a race since then.

12. **12 countries.** — The passage states that only 300 athletes from 12 countries went to Athens in 1896.

13. **Since 1928 (the Amsterdam Olympics).** — Female athletes participated in the Olympic Games for the first time in 1928 in Amsterdam.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **C) Someone who plays a sport without earning money from it** — The text defines an amateur as someone who does not earn any money from sport, unlike a professional sportsman.

2. **D) They have grown in popularity and expanded to include women.** — The text explains that in 1896 only 300 athletes from 12 countries competed, and "since then, of course, the games have become much more popular," while also noting the addition of female athletes in 1928.

3. **A) Government financial support makes it harder to keep the games purely amateur and non-political.** — The author notes that although Olympic athletes are supposed to be amateurs, government funding for their training and travel blurs the line between amateur and professional sport and brings politics into the games.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Origin and Growth of the Olympic Marathon):**

The marathon got its name from a Greek village where a famous war happened in 490 BC. After the Greeks won, a soldier ran over 40 kilometres from Marathon to Athens. In 1896, the first modern Olympic Games in Athens included the marathon to remember this event. At first, only 300 male athletes from 12 countries competed, and women first joined in 1928. Today, although Olympic athletes are called amateurs, governments often pay for their training and travel, which makes some people call the games a political marathon.


**Task 2 (Government Support for Olympic Athletes):**

In my opinion, governments should support Olympic athletes, because training for world-class sport requires a lot of time and money. Most Olympic athletes are not rich professionals; many are students or teachers who cannot pay for travel, equipment, and coaching by themselves. Without government help, talented young people from poorer backgrounds would never be able to compete. However, countries should focus on fair play and sportsmanship rather than using the games for political pride. When athletes are supported fairly, the Olympic Games remain an inspiring celebration of human effort and international friendship.


---


## Passage 85: The Technological Revolution (`raw1_085` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **2000**

2. **bills**

3. **100,000 / one hundred thousand**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **B) To process data on soil chemistry, insect control, and plant genetics** — Farmers have been using computers to process information about soil chemistry, insect control, and plant genetics.

2. **C) Workers are freed from physical work to use their brains for new discoveries.** — Because technology and computerized robots supply the muscle power on assembly lines, workers are free to use their brain power to make new discoveries.

3. **A) Designing car shapes and calculating engine part sizes** — In the automobile industry, computer graphics help engineers design the shape of the car and calculate the size of engine parts.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — The passage states that business managers use computers to do calculations in seconds that are beyond the power of the human brain.

2. **Not Given** — The passage says computerized robots have replaced many workers on assembly lines, but it does not state whether unemployment overall has increased or decreased.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **False** — Tractors and threshers have taken the place of field workers so that one person can feed thousands, reducing the need for farm workers.

2. **True** — The passage states that computers do calculations "beyond the power of the human brain."

3. **True** — In the automobile industry, computer graphics help engineers design the shape of the car and calculate the size of engine parts.

4. **False** — The final paragraph explicitly states that the human brain has not changed in size in the past 100,000 years.

5. **To process information about soil chemistry, insect control, and plant genetics.** — Paragraph 2 states that farmers use computers to process information in these three areas.

6. **They do the work of accountants and secretaries (typing hundreds of letters and sending bills worldwide by satellite transmission), collect large amounts of information, and perform complex calculations quickly for managers.** — Paragraph 3 describes these office, communication, and analytical roles of computers in business.

7. **Workers are free to use their brain power to make new discoveries because technology supplies the muscle power.** — Paragraph 4 explains that replacing physical labour with robots allows workers to focus on thinking and discovery.

8. **man** — "Him" refers back to "man" earlier in the same sentence ("man has developed the power to change the world around him").

9. **modern tractors and threshers** — "Their help" refers to the help of the modern tractors and threshers mentioned in the previous sentence.

10. **workers** — "Their" refers to "workers" ("workers are free to use their brain power").


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **B) How computers and modern machines are transforming farming, business, and industry** — The passage surveys how machines and computers are transforming agriculture, business, and industry while freeing human brain power.

2. **D) Physical strength and manual labour** — "Technology supplies the muscle power" contrasts with "brain power", meaning machines provide the physical labour and strength previously done by human workers.

3. **A) Human creativity does not depend on the brain growing physically larger.** — Even though the human brain has stayed the same size for 100,000 years, humans have invented increasingly advanced tools—from hand tools to steam engines to computers.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Technology Across Three Sectors):**

Modern technology and computers are transforming farming, business, and industry. On farms, tractors, threshers, and computers help a single farmer produce food for thousands by analysing soil, insects, and genetics. In offices, computers handle typing, global billing, and complex calculations much faster than humans. In factories, robots take over heavy physical work on assembly lines, while computer graphics help engineers design cars. Although the human brain has not grown in size for 100,000 years, humans continue to invent intelligent machines to do difficult work.


**Task 2 (Machines Replacing Human Workers):**

Although computers and robots replace many traditional jobs, they also create valuable opportunities for society. In the short term, automation can make life difficult for factory workers, secretaries, and farm labourers whose tasks are taken over by machines. If people lose their jobs without support, unemployment can become a serious social problem. On the other hand, technology removes dangerous and repetitive physical work and increases productivity. As the passage suggests, workers freed from manual labour can use their creativity and intelligence to solve new problems and develop new industries. Therefore, society should invest in education and job retraining so that workers can adapt to new roles. Overall, intelligent machines are beneficial as long as people are prepared to work alongside them.


---


## Passage 86: Manchester (`raw1_086` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **cotton trade**

2. **thirty-five miles / 35 miles**

3. **seventy-one / 71**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) It is made into cloth in Lancashire and exported worldwide.** — The narrator states that cotton is not grown in Lancashire, but made into cloth there and exported worldwide.

2. **B) Transporting goods overland to Liverpool increased costs and reduced profits.** — Before the canal, goods had to be transported over thirty miles to Liverpool and loaded onto ships, which increased costs and reduced profits.

3. **D) Building bridges for railway lines and controlling rivers and streams** — Engineers had to build bridges for five railway lines and prevent rivers and streams from flowing into the canal and changing the water-level.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **False** — The passage states that great ships load or unload cargo at the port every hour of the day and night.

2. **True** — Seventy-one ships sailed on January 1st, 1894, while Queen Victoria sailed up the waterway for the official opening ceremony on May 21st of the same year.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **c) more than thirty miles away from Liverpool** — Paragraph 2 states that cotton goods had to be transported to Liverpool, 'over thirty miles away'.

2. **d) working in factories** — Lancashire is a huge industrial area where cloth, soap, chemicals, dyes, rubber goods, paper goods, and machinery are manufactured. Cotton is not grown there.

3. **c) is the most important city for buying and selling goods after London** — 'A great trade centre, second only to London' means London ranks first in trade and Manchester ranks second.

4. **d) transported to Liverpool to be loaded on ships** — Paragraph 2 states that before the canal was built, cotton goods had to be transported to Liverpool and loaded onto cargo ships there.

5. **d) There are many industrial towns in Lancashire.** — Paragraph 1 mentions many smaller towns around Manchester in Lancashire where cotton, soap, chemicals, dyes, rubber, and paper goods are manufactured.

6. **d) the engineers who built it were skilful and they managed to deal with the serious problems they met** — Perseverance means continuing firmly in spite of difficulties; the engineers solved difficult challenges like crossing railway lines and controlling rivers.

7. **a) it allows large ships to sail into the port of Manchester** — The canal made overland transport to Liverpool unnecessary by letting large ships sail directly to Manchester safely and easily.

8. **b) is nearer the sea than Manchester** — Liverpool stands at the mouth of the River Mersey, whereas Manchester is an inland city thirty-five miles farther upstream.

9. **the cotton goods** — Cargo ships at Liverpool carried the cotton goods all over the world.

10. **the Manchester Ship Canal (the canal)** — Docks, warehouses, stores, and factories were built along the banks (sides) of the Manchester Ship Canal.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **B) How the Manchester Ship Canal transformed Manchester's trade and industry** — The passage mainly explains how the construction of the Manchester Ship Canal turned an inland industrial city into a major port and solved its transport problems.

2. **C) Keeping a controlled water level was essential for the canal to operate safely.** — Rivers and streams could not flow into the canal because they would interfere with the water-level, indicating that ships need a stable, controlled water level to pass safely.

3. **A) appeared and grew rapidly** — Huge docks, warehouses, stores, and factories 'sprung up' along the banks, meaning they appeared and developed quickly once the canal was built.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summary: The Impact of the Manchester Ship Canal):**

Manchester is a major industrial city in Lancashire famous for cotton cloth, machinery, and various manufactured goods. Previously, exporters had to transport goods over thirty miles to Liverpool to load them onto ships, which raised costs and lowered profits. In 1894, the thirty-five-mile Manchester Ship Canal solved this problem. Despite obstacles like railway crossings and rivers, engineers completed the canal, transforming Manchester from an inland city into one of Britain's busiest ports.


**Task 2 (Opinion Paragraph: Investing in Transportation Infrastructure):**

Major engineering projects such as canals, bridges, and railways play a vital role in a country's economic development. First, they reduce transportation costs and save time when moving raw materials and finished products between cities and ports. Just as the Manchester Ship Canal increased Manchester's trade profits, modern transport links help businesses reach global markets more efficiently. Furthermore, large infrastructure projects create new jobs and encourage factories and warehouses to grow along their routes. Although building them requires great skill, money, and perseverance, their long-term benefits for trade and local communities make them well worth the investment.


---


## Passage 87: 'SEN' OR 'SİZ'? (`raw1_087` · `A2`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **first names**

2. **thou**

3. **another choice**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) They consider it very impolite and feel uncomfortable.** — The passage explains that many older people feel uncomfortable because calling them by their first names is considered very impolite in many countries.

2. **A) 'sen' and 'tu'** — In Turkish, 'sen' is informal and 'siz' is formal; in French, 'tu' is familiar and 'vous' is more formal.

3. **D) Their relationship has changed from formal to informal.** — Switching from 'vous' to 'tu' shows that a relationship in France has moved from a formal one to an informal one.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — The passage states that young people in Turkey and France usually use the informal form when talking to each other.

2. **False** — The passage says that English speakers such as Australians, Americans, and the British never have to choose between two forms because English only has 'you'.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **your parents** — The question asks whether you call your parents 'Mother', 'Father', 'Mum', 'Dad', or by their first names.

2. **many young children (in "their parents"), or the parents and parents' friends (in "by their first names")** — In "call their parents, and their parents' friends, by their first names", the first two occurrences of 'their' refer to 'many young children', while the final 'their' refers to the parents and parents' friends.

3. **'you'** — Paragraph 2 explains that French and Turkish have two forms for the word 'you', which 'the word' refers back to.

4. **Turkey and France** — 'Both countries' refers directly back to 'Turkey and France' in the previous sentence.

5. **English speaking people (Australians, Americans and the British)** — 'They' refers to the subject of the sentence: English speaking people (Australians, Americans, and the British).

6. **True** — Paragraph 1 says that calling parents and parents' friends by their first names is very impolite in many countries and makes older people uncomfortable.

7. **False** — Paragraph 2 says that two forms of 'you' exist in 'some languages, such as French and Turkish', not in most languages.

8. **False** — Paragraph 3 states that English speaking people never have this difficulty because there is only one form ('you') in modern English.

9. **A few hundred years ago.** — Paragraph 2 states: 'a few hundred years ago there was also the formal expression "thou".'

10. **When young people are talking to each other (or in France, when a relationship changes from formal to informal).** — Paragraph 3 states that young people usually use the informal form when talking to each other, and in France changing from 'vous' to 'tu' reflects a shift to an informal relationship.

11. **Using 'vous' instead of 'tu' keeps or shows a formal relationship, whereas changing from 'vous' to 'tu' shows a change from a formal relationship to an informal one.** — Paragraph 3 explains that in France, the change from 'vous' to 'tu' shows a change from a formal relationship to an informal one; therefore, using 'vous' instead of 'tu' indicates a formal relationship.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **B) How different languages and cultures handle formal and informal ways of addressing people** — The passage compares how polite and informal forms of address—such as first names and different forms of 'you'—are used in different languages and cultures.

2. **A) a formal form of 'you' such as 'siz' or 'vous'** — The passage says it is often unacceptable for an older person to be called 'sen' or 'tu' by a younger person, implying younger speakers should use 'siz' or 'vous' out of respect.

3. **C) informal and close behaviour that may seem disrespectful** — 'Familiarity' refers to treating older people informally, like close friends, by calling them by their first names.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summary: Formal and Informal Forms of Address):**

The passage explains how people address others in different cultures. Calling parents or older people by their first names is seen as impolite in many countries. Unlike modern English, which only uses 'you', languages such as Turkish and French have two forms: informal ('sen' and 'tu') and formal ('siz' and 'vous'). Younger people use the informal form with each other, while older people usually expect the formal one.


**Task 2 (Opinion Paragraph: Formal vs. Informal 'You'):**

I think having both formal and informal forms of 'you' is very useful in a language. First, it helps speakers show respect immediately to older people, teachers, or strangers without needing extra words. For example, in Turkish, saying 'siz' politely signals distance and respect. Second, switching from 'siz' to 'sen' shows that two people have become closer friends. Although learners sometimes find it difficult to choose the right form, these two options make communication clearer and more polite.


---


## Passage 88: Vive la Difference! (`raw1_088` · `A2`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **150 countries / one hundred and fifty countries**

2. **both cheeks**

3. **camels**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **B) The money people use and the food they eat** — The passage lists what people look like, the different money they use, and the different food they eat as differences a visitor sees immediately.

2. **C) People cut their meat into small pieces first and then eat only with a fork.** — In the United States, people cut their meat into small pieces first and then eat using only a fork.

3. **A) Cricket is popular in Britain, though not in the rest of Europe.** — The passage says that cricket is popular in Britain, while it is not popular in the rest of Europe.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **False** — In Germany, it is considered impolite to cut potatoes with a knife, not polite.

2. **True** — The passage states that you learn about the less obvious differences when you get to know the people of the country well.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **countries** — The previous sentences talk about countries ('There are more than 150 countries... Some of them are very big...'), so 'Others' means other countries.

2. **some differences you cannot see immediately** — 'These' refers back to 'some differences you cannot see immediately' in the preceding sentence.

3. **saying something to each other before starting to eat** — In many countries people say something like 'Guten Appetit' before starting to eat, whereas in Britain this (saying something before starting to eat) is not the custom.

4. **people's (people)** — 'Their' refers back to 'people' in the same sentence.

5. **False** — There are more than 150 countries in total; some are very big, while others are smaller than some cities.

6. **True** — Paragraph 1 states that people in different countries have their own customs (social habits), traditions, languages, and beliefs.

7. **True** — Paragraph 3 states that British people only shake hands with people they meet for the first time and usually only kiss close friends and relatives.

8. **False** — Paragraph 4 states that Americans cut their meat into small pieces before starting to eat (using a knife) and then eat only with a fork.

9. **True** — Paragraph 4 explicitly says 'in many countries people don't eat with knives or forks at all'.

10. **False** — Paragraph 5 states that cricket is popular in Britain 'but not in the rest of Europe'.

11. **True** — The final sentence states: 'All these differences between countries make travel interesting.'

12. **a) What people look like, b) the different money they use, c) the different food they eat.** — Paragraph 2 lists these three things as obvious differences a visitor immediately sees.

13. **a) The way people greet their friends (greeting customs), b) customs linked with eating (eating habits), c) what people do in their spare time (spare-time activities and sports).** — Paragraphs 3, 4, and 5 describe these three less obvious cultural differences: greeting, eating, and spare-time activities.

14. **Countries around the world have many visible and hidden cultural differences—such as customs in greeting, eating, and spare-time activities—which make travel interesting.** — The passage introduces obvious and hidden cultural differences across countries and concludes that these differences make travel interesting.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **C) To give examples of how table manners and eating habits differ around the world** — Paragraph 4 gives several examples of eating customs in different countries, such as saying 'Guten Appetit', how forks and knives are used, and eating without utensils.

2. **D) Understanding social customs takes time and closer contact with local people.** — Visitors see food and money right away, whereas greeting habits, table manners, and spare-time customs take time to learn by getting to know the people.

3. **B) free time** — 'Spare time' refers to free time in the evening or at the weekend when people are not working.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summary: Cultural Differences Around the World):**

Countries around the world differ in many ways. Some differences, like appearance, money, and food, are easy for visitors to notice immediately. Other differences only become clear when you know the people well. These include greeting habits, such as kissing cheeks in France versus shaking hands in Britain, eating customs in countries like Germany and the United States, and spare-time sports like cricket, camel racing, and Sumo wrestling. Together, these differences make travelling exciting.


**Task 2 (Opinion Paragraph: Respecting Local Customs While Travelling):**

Learning about another country's customs before travelling is very helpful for several reasons. First, it prevents misunderstandings and helps you avoid accidentally behaving in an impolite way, especially during meals or greetings. For example, in Turkey, guests are expected to greet older people respectfully and often take off their shoes before entering a home. When visitors know and follow such traditions, local people feel respected and welcoming. In addition, understanding local habits and popular sports makes a trip much richer and more enjoyable.


---


## Passage 89: Tornadoes (`raw1_089` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **vacuum cleaner**

2. **30 seconds / thirty seconds**

3. **689**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) More than 800 kilometres per hour** — The script states that the wind speed of a tornado may be more than 800 kilometres per hour.

2. **B) Hot, humid days** — According to the passage, tornadoes normally happen on hot, humid days, though not necessarily in the summer.

3. **D) In an underground cellar or basement** — The script advises that the safest place to be during a tornado is an underground area like a cellar or a basement.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **False** — The script says tornadoes have carried people and animals hundreds of metres, often without causing physical harm.

2. **True** — The script mentions that more than 200 tornadoes strike the United States every year.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **About 20 to 30 minutes on the average (with the greatest damage in one place lasting only about 30 seconds).** — Paragraph 3 states: "Fortunately, a tornado does not last long, about 20 to 30 minutes on the average."

2. **Hot, humid days (not necessarily in the summer).** — Paragraph 3 states: "Tornadoes normally occur on hot, humid days but not necessarily in the summer."

3. **An underground area such as a cellar or a basement.** — Paragraph 4 states: "The safest place to be if a tornado seems likely is in some underground area such as a cellar or a basement."

4. **Any two examples from the text, such as driving wheat straw into posts and trees, turning buildings around on their foundations, carrying away cars, trucks, or freight trains, or destroying an area about 26 kilometres long.** — Paragraphs 2 and 3 provide several specific examples of physical destruction and strange damage caused by tornadoes.

5. **False** — The wind speed of a tornado may exceed 800 kilometres per hour; 96 kilometres per hour was the forward travel speed of the 1925 tornado.

6. **False** — The passage states that tornadoes occur on hot, humid days "but not necessarily in the summer."

7. **True** — Paragraph 2 mentions that buildings "have been turned completely around on their foundations."

8. **False** — Paragraph 4 states that "More than 200 tornadoes strike in the United States every year."

9. **True** — The most destructive tornado struck on March 18, 1925, which falls in March (spring).

10. **True** — Paragraph 4 explains that it is still not possible to predict when a tornado will strike, even though storm warnings are issued when conditions are right.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **C) To illustrate the extraordinary and strange force of tornadoes** — Paragraph 2 illustrates the unbelievable and bizarre strength of tornadoes by listing unusual incidents such as straw embedded in trees, intact rotated buildings, and lifted freight trains.

2. **B) Undamaged and in one piece** — Saying buildings were turned around 'and have remained intact' means they stayed undamaged and in one piece despite being moved.

3. **A) Officials can tell when conditions favour a tornado, not the exact moment it will hit** — The passage states it is not possible to predict when a tornado will strike, even though warnings can be issued when weather conditions are suitable.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summarizing Tornado Facts):**

Tornadoes are extremely powerful storms whose funnel-shaped winds can exceed 800 kilometres per hour, lifting heavy objects such as cars, trains, and buildings. Although they usually last only 20 to 30 minutes, they cause intense damage in a short time. Over 200 tornadoes hit the United States annually, typically on hot, humid days. While exact prediction remains impossible, weather warnings allow people to shelter safely underground in basements or cellars.


**Task 2 (Preparedness Against Unpredictable Storms):**

Although scientists cannot predict the exact moment a tornado will strike, modern warning systems still save many lives. When meteorologists identify hot, humid conditions that could produce a storm, alerts give residents precious minutes to reach a cellar or basement. In addition, public education teaches people what to do when a siren sounds instead of panicking. Even though tornadoes remain dangerous and unpredictable, combining early warnings with prepared shelters significantly reduces injuries and deaths.


---


## Passage 90: Industrial Medicine and Safety (`raw1_090` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **black lung disease / black lung**

2. **deafness**

3. **cover their ears**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **B) Industrial doctors and safety engineers** — The passage states that both industrial doctors and safety engineers educate workers about job dangers and workplace safety.

2. **C) The skin, lungs, and internal organs** — According to the passage, new toxic chemicals can harm the skin, the lungs, and internal organs like the heart, liver, or kidney.

3. **A) Eye goggles, hard hats, and safety shoes** — The script lists eye goggles, hard hats, and safety shoes as examples of protective clothing.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **False** — The script explicitly notes that lead, mercury, and silica made people sick in the past and still make people sick today, alongside newer chemicals.

2. **Not Given** — The passage states that large companies employ industrial doctors and safety engineers, but it does not mention whether small companies are legally required to hire them.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **d) Companies protect workers from accidents and illness** — The preceding sentence states that large companies employ industrial doctors and safety engineers "to protect workers from accidents and illness on the job," and "They do this in three ways" introduces how that protection is carried out.

2. **a) causes** — "a few" refers back to "many causes of accidents and illness at work" in the previous sentence.

3. **c) hurts** — In this context, toxic chemicals harming internal organs means they hurt or damage them.

4. **c) hear** — Deafness is the condition of being unable to hear, which can be caused by loud industrial noise.

5. **True** — Paragraph 1 explains that lung cancer is common among asbestos workers, coal miners develop black lung disease, and they probably get these diseases because of their work.

6. **True** — Paragraph 1 notes that accidents may injure or kill workers, mentioning farm machines injuring farm workers and construction workers falling from buildings with fatal injuries.

7. **False** — Paragraph 2 specifies that doctors study workers and their diseases, while safety engineers study accidents and their causes.

8. **True** — Paragraph 2 states that they educate workers about safety on the job and that "Education is necessary for prevention."

9. **False** — Paragraph 3 notes that lead, mercury, and silica made people sick in the past as well as today, contrasting them with "new chemicals."

10. **False** — Paragraph 4 explains that they design new machines *after* finding the causes of accidents, in order to prevent accidents in the future.

11. **True** — Paragraph 4 lists designing different ways to store and transport toxic chemicals as one of the key preventive actions.

12. **False** — Paragraph 4 states that "Some workers must wear protective clothing," not everybody in large companies.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **C) To explain workplace health risks and how specialists prevent them** — The passage explains common workplace diseases and accidents, and then focuses on how industrial doctors and safety engineers investigate, redesign, educate, and set standards to protect workers.

2. **B) Causing death** — Paragraph 1 gives examples of accidents that kill workers ("kill workers... fall from buildings. These injuries can be fatal"), showing that "fatal" means causing death.

3. **D) Doctors focus on work-related illnesses while safety engineers focus on accidents, and both cooperate on prevention** — The text states that doctors study workers' diseases while safety engineers study accidents and their causes, and then both work together to modify machines, educate workers, and set safety standards.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Protecting Workers from Occupational Hazards):**

Workers in hazardous industries face both job-related illnesses from chemicals, noise, and radiation and physical accidents from machinery, falls, or fires. To reduce these risks, many large companies employ industrial doctors and safety engineers. Together, they research causes, redesign dangerous machines and chemical storage methods, set safety standards such as protective clothing, and educate employees. Through study, engineering, and training, they make workplaces much safer.


**Task 2 (Rules vs. Education in Workplace Safety):**

Both safety rules and worker education are essential for a safe workplace, as neither works well alone. Companies must set strict standards, design safer machines, and provide protective gear like hard hats and goggles so that dangerous environments are physically controlled. However, rules can fail if employees do not understand why they matter or how to follow them properly. When workers receive clear safety training, they are much more likely to spot hazards early and use equipment correctly. Therefore, combining strong workplace regulations with continuous education offers the best protection against accidents and illnesses.


---


## Passage 91: Learning About Computers (`raw1_091` · `A2`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **on and off switches / switches**

2. **records**

3. **existing programme / existing program**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **A) The CPU** — The script calls the CPU, or central processing unit, the heart of the computer.

2. **C) It displays typed text or saved information from memory** — According to the script, the monitor shows either new information we have typed or stored information requested from memory.

3. **B) Teaching French and playing games** — The script gives learning French and playing games as examples of what different computer programmes do.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — The script says the only way to learn to use a computer is to actually do it, after knowing what the different parts do.

2. **False** — The script states that programming is the job of specialists and regular users do not need to know how to programme.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **Any two of the following: it can help us learn fast, work successfully, have a good time, or store very large amounts of information for easy reference.** — Paragraph 1 lists these ways a computer can assist users.

2. **It saves information you want to keep.** — Under CPU, the passage states: "and the memory, which saves information you want to keep."

3. **False** — The passage states that "The hardware is the computer machinery," whereas software is the computer programme.

4. **False** — The keyboard has keys that look like those on a typewriter; the CPU consists of billions of on and off switches.

5. **True** — Under MONITOR, the text explains that it shows new information we have typed or information requested from memory.

6. **False** — The printer is listed as a separate hardware part alongside the CPU, keyboard, disks/disk drive, and monitor; the CPU contains the processor and memory.

7. **True** — Under SOFTWARE, the passage notes: "some programmes can teach us French."

8. **False** — The final paragraph explains that specialists programme computers, while regular users only need to learn how to use an existing programme.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **B) To change information into a form people can understand** — The text explicitly states that the processor changes information into a form that people can understand, while the memory saves information.

2. **D) Keep** — The passage states that a computer can "store, or keep, very large amounts of information," directly defining "store" as "keep."

3. **A) Hardware refers to the physical machine parts, while software refers to the programmes that direct them** — The passage explains that hardware is the computer machinery, whereas software consists of programmes made of codes that tell the computer how to perform tasks.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Hardware and Software Basics):**

A computer system has two main elements: hardware and software. Hardware includes physical parts such as the CPU for processing and saving data, the keyboard for typing information, disks for storage, the monitor for viewing text, and the printer for putting work on paper. Software consists of programmes that tell the computer what tasks to perform, and ordinary users only need to learn how to use existing programmes.


**Task 2 (Using Computers for Learning):**

I think computers are very helpful tools for learning new things. First, students can find large amounts of information quickly for school projects instead of searching through many heavy books. Second, special learning programmes and educational games make studying subjects like mathematics or foreign languages more interesting. Although people should also take breaks from the screen, using a computer regularly helps students study faster and prepare for modern jobs.


---


## Passage 92: Seat Belts (`raw1_092` · `A2`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **50 pounds / fifty pounds**

2. **children**

3. **backing / reversing**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) The person sitting in the middle front seat** — The passage states that if two passengers sit in the front, the person sitting in the middle does not have to wear a seat belt.

2. **B) Get a medical certificate from a doctor** — According to the passage, certain people can get a medical certificate from a doctor if wearing a seat belt could be worse for their health.

3. **D) Keep it and show it to the police if asked** — The passage advises keeping the medical certificate and showing it to the police if they ask why you are not wearing a seat belt.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **False** — If just one passenger sits in the front of a vehicle with a middle front seat, that passenger must wear a seat belt.

2. **Not Given** — The passage only mentions rules for drivers and front seat passengers; it does not mention back seat passengers or fines for them.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **b) a middle front seat** — The previous sentence describes vehicles that have a middle front seat, so 'them' refers to vehicles with a middle front seat.

2. **d) shouldn't wear seat belts because of their health** — Earlier in the paragraph, the passage states that certain people should not wear a seat belt because of their health, and 'these people' refers back to them.

3. **False** — Passengers aged 14 or over are responsible for wearing their own seat belts; the driver is only responsible for making sure children under 14 wear one.

4. **False** — Simply talking to a doctor is not enough; people are exempt only if they actually obtain a medical certificate from a doctor.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **C) Rules and exceptions for wearing seat belts in vehicles** — The passage explains who has to wear a seat belt, when exceptions apply, and what documents are needed.

2. **B) The 16-year-old passenger can be fined up to £50.** — A 16-year-old is 14 or over, so it is their own responsibility to wear a seat belt, and they can be fined up to £50 if they do not.

3. **A) Driving your vehicle backwards** — In this context, 'backing your vehicle' means driving it backwards (reversing).


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Seat Belt Rules Summary):**

Most drivers and front seat passengers have to wear a seat belt. Passengers aged 14 or over are responsible for their own belts, while drivers must make sure children under 14 wear one. However, there are some exceptions. People do not need to wear a belt when backing a vehicle, sitting in the middle front seat with another passenger, or if they have a medical certificate from a doctor.


**Task 2 (Should Everyone Wear a Seat Belt?):**

I think wearing a seat belt is one of the easiest ways to stay safe in a car. Even if someone is a very careful driver, accidents can happen suddenly because of other drivers or bad weather. A seat belt keeps passengers in their seats and reduces the risk of serious injuries. In my opinion, all passengers, including people in the back seat, should always wear a seat belt on every journey, short or long.


---


## Passage 93: James Dean (`raw1_093` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **marmalade**

2. **bongo drums / drums**

3. **half a million / 500,000**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) While Dean was acting in a play in New York** — The famous director Elia Kazan saw Dean acting in a play in New York and realised he would be perfect for East of Eden.

2. **A) He found Dean angry and difficult to be with.** — Rock Hudson said Dean was hard to be with, was always angry, and never smiled, so he did not like working with him.

3. **B) The light was poor and he was not wearing his glasses.** — On 25 September 1955, the light was poor as the sun was going down, and Dean was not wearing his glasses when he hit a slowing car at high speed.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — Dean sat in a corner at parties, took off his glasses because he did not want to see anybody, and played the drums all night without speaking.

2. **Not Given** — The passage states that he loved riding on fast motorbikes and driving sports cars, but does not mention whether he won any races.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **c) has trouble with his eyes** — The sentence immediately explains that he could not really see anything without his glasses.

2. **a) has ideas and habits that other people find strange** — The paragraph illustrates Dean's eccentric behaviour by describing his unusual clothes, silence for days, and habit of playing drums alone in corners at parties.

3. **True** — Dean went to New York, got a part in a play, and was seen there by Elia Kazan, who chose him for East of Eden.

4. **True** — Rock Hudson described Dean as hard to be with, always angry, and someone who never smiled.

5. **False** — The two German girls were not in the 1955 car accident; they killed themselves after hearing the news of Dean's death.

6. **False** — Dean acted in three films (East of Eden, Rebel Without a Cause, and Giant). Jimmy Dean Returns was a book written a few years after his death.

7. **Giant.** — The passage notes that Dean made East of Eden, Rebel Without a Cause, and Giant, and says 'His co-star in the last film, Rock Hudson...'.

8. **a) The sun was going down, so the light was poor. b) He was not wearing his glasses.** — The fifth paragraph states that the sun was going down so the light was poor, and unfortunately Dean was not wearing his glasses.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **D) Getting a theatre role in New York and meeting Elia Kazan** — Dean's career really started after he moved to New York, got a role in a play, and was spotted by director Elia Kazan.

2. **C) Dean's death inspired extreme reactions and an enduring legend among fans.** — Both the tragic suicides of two fans and the huge sales of a book claiming to contact him from the other world show the intense, unusual devotion surrounding Dean after his death.

3. **B) Survive and pay for basic needs with** — In 'for a time he had to live on very little money', 'live on' means to survive or support oneself using a particular amount of money or food.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Biography Summary: James Dean):**

James Dean moved to Hollywood at eighteen, struggled at first, and later achieved success after director Elia Kazan discovered him in a New York play. Although Dean starred in three famous films, he was eccentric and hard to work with. On 25 September 1955, he died in a high-speed car crash while driving without his glasses. His sudden death created a lasting legend among fans around the world.


**Task 2 (The Legend of Young Stars):**

Young actors or musicians who die suddenly often become legends because people remember them as forever young and talented. Fans never see them grow old or make less successful work, so their image stays powerful and mysterious. In addition, media stories, books, and movies keep their memory alive for new generations. However, extreme fan behaviour can sometimes be unhealthy when people confuse an actor's screen image with real life. Overall, remembering an artist's work is wonderful, as long as fans keep a healthy balance.


---


## Passage 94: Skyscrapers (`raw1_094` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **1884**

2. **windows**

3. **528**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **B) Chicago** — Although most people guess New York City, the passage clearly states that Chicago was the birthplace of the skyscraper.

2. **C) It was pulled down in 1931.** — The Home Insurance Company Building was pulled down in 1931, so it no longer exists.

3. **D) Enough money and protection of the environment** — The passage states that architects and engineers have the technology, but must also find the money and make sure new skyscrapers do not harm the environment.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — People on a high floor can see a sunny sky while those on downstairs floors watch the rain from their windows.

2. **False** — The passage states that Wright's dream of a 528-floor skyscraper near Lake Michigan never became a reality.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **d) the other early skyscrapers in Chicago** — The preceding sentence refers to 'other early Chicago skyscrapers', and the Reliance Building is given as one example of them.

2. **a) was the first skyscraper in the U.S.A.** — The passage says it was the first building to fit the definition of a skyscraper, built in Chicago in 1884 with 10 storeys and pulled down in 1931.

3. **b) can experience different weather conditions** — People on a high floor can look out on a sunny sky while those on downstairs floors watch the rain from their windows.

4. **False** — It had a strong framework of iron and steel instead of walls of stone.

5. **True** — The passage states that besides having the technology, builders must find the money and ensure new skyscrapers do not harm the environment.

6. **False** — Frank Lloyd Wright planned it in 1956, but his dream never became a reality.

7. **True** — The first paragraph explicitly states that Chicago was the birthplace of the skyscraper.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **C) Supporting the building with an iron and steel framework instead of stone walls** — Using a strong iron and steel skeleton instead of stone walls made it possible to build much taller structures like the Home Insurance Company Building.

2. **A) To correct a common misconception about where skyscrapers originated** — The author points out that most people wrongly guess New York City because New York later led in skyscraper construction, even though Chicago actually came first.

3. **B) Demolished or tore it down** — The passage says 'they pulled it down in 1931' and then contrasts that with 'visitors to the city can still see other early Chicago skyscrapers', meaning the building was demolished.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (The Birth and Growth of Skyscrapers):**

Although many people think of New York, the skyscraper was born in Chicago with the 10-storey Home Insurance Company Building in 1884. It used an iron and steel skeleton instead of stone walls. Later landmarks included the glass-covered Reliance Building and the 100-floor John Hancock Center. Today, builders have the technology for taller towers, but they must consider cost and the environment.


**Task 2 (Should Cities Build Taller Skyscrapers?):**

Building extremely tall skyscrapers in modern cities has both benefits and drawbacks. On the positive side, skyscrapers save valuable land in crowded city centres and provide homes and offices for thousands of people in one place. They can also become famous landmarks that attract tourists. On the other hand, as the passage notes, tall buildings are very expensive to construct and can harm the environment by using huge amounts of energy and resources. In my opinion, cities should only build taller skyscrapers when they are truly needed and designed to be environmentally friendly.


---


## Passage 95: Temperate Climates (`raw1_095` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **6 degrees Celsius / six degrees Celsius / 6°C**

2. **winds**

3. **maritime zone**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **B) It has dry, hot summers and mild, wet winters.** — The speaker explains that the western or Mediterranean type has dry, hot summers and mild, wet winters, and is found on western coasts and across southern Europe.

2. **C) Up to five months a year** — According to the passage, cool temperate zones can have as many as five months a year with average temperatures below 6 degrees Celsius.

3. **A) Warmer summers and cold winters** — The continental region is described as having warmer summers and cold winters, unlike the maritime zone which has frequent weather changes.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — The passage states that temperate climates are free from very high and very low temperatures.

2. **Not Given** — The passage discusses where the Mediterranean type is found and how plants grow in warm zones, but it does not mention which climate type has the largest human population.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **6°C (the lowest average temperature in the coldest month of warm temperate zones)** — In the preceding sentence, the passage notes that the average temperature does not drop below 6°C, and 'This' refers to 6°C.

2. **the eastern (or China) type of warm temperate climate** — The previous sentence introduces 'the eastern or China type', which 'This kind of climate' refers back to.

3. **Warm temperate zones: western (Mediterranean) type and eastern (China) type. Cool temperate zones: maritime zone and continental region.** — The text divides temperate climates into warm temperate zones (western/Mediterranean and eastern/China) and cool temperate zones (maritime and continental).

4. **A climate that is free from very high and very low temperatures.** — The first paragraph explicitly defines temperate climates as climates free from very high and very low temperatures.

5. **On the western coasts and across southern Europe.** — Paragraph 2 states that the western or Mediterranean type is found on the western coasts and across southern Europe.

6. **As many as five months a year.** — Paragraph 3 says there are as many as five months a year when the average temperature goes below 6°C in cool temperate zones.

7. **The maritime zone.** — Paragraph 3 identifies the maritime zone as having frequent changes in weather conditions.

8. **It has a cold season (up to five months below 6°C), with warmer summers and cold winters.** — Paragraph 3 describes the continental region as a cool temperate zone with warmer summers and cold winters.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **C) To classify and describe the main types of temperate climates** — The article systematically defines temperate climates and categorizes them into warm (western/eastern) and cool (maritime/continental) zones.

2. **B) Plants cannot grow all year round in cool temperate zones.** — Because warm temperate zones never drop below 6°C—the minimum temperature needed to grow plants all year round—cool zones with five months below 6°C cannot support year-round plant growth.

3. **D) damp and moist** — In the context of 'hot, humid summers' contrasted with 'dry, hot summers' in the Mediterranean type, 'humid' refers to air containing a lot of moisture or dampness.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summarizing Temperate Climate Zones):**

Temperate climates have moderate temperatures without extreme heat or cold and are split into warm and cool zones. Warm temperate zones stay above 6°C all year, allowing continuous plant growth. They include the Mediterranean type, which has dry summers and mild, wet winters, and the wetter China type, shaped by winds with humid summers. In contrast, cool temperate zones drop below 6°C for up to five months and consist of maritime zones with changeable weather and continental regions with cold winters and warmer summers.


**Task 2 (Choosing Your Ideal Temperate Climate):**

Although every climate has its advantages, I would prefer to live in a warm temperate Mediterranean climate rather than a cool temperate continental region. First, mild and wet winters make daily life much more comfortable and reduce the need for heavy heating compared to harsh continental winters. Second, because the average temperature never drops below 6°C, local farmers and gardeners can grow fresh vegetables, fruits, and flowers throughout the entire year. This creates greener surroundings and encourages an active outdoor lifestyle. Even though summers in the Mediterranean zone are hot and dry, people can easily adapt by staying indoors during midday hours or spending time near the coast. By contrast, enduring up to five cold months each year in a continental climate limits outdoor activities and increases energy costs. Overall, a Mediterranean climate offers a healthier and more pleasant balance across all seasons.


---


## Passage 96: The Pen (`raw1_096` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **bone or metal**

2. **bamboo**

3. **1884**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) The Latin word for feather** — The speaker explains that the quill pen was made from a large bird feather, and the Latin word for feather is 'penna', which gave us the word 'pen'.

2. **A) It needed to be sharpened quite often.** — Because the quill was made from a feather, it wore down and had to be sharpened frequently using a special tool called a penknife.

3. **D) Laszlo Biro's ball-point pen** — The passage ends by stating that in 1943, Laszlo Biro, an Argentinian, invented the ball-point pen, which became even more popular than the fountain pen.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — The passage states that the stylus was used for thousands of years and was very practical for marking stone or wood.

2. **False** — In the 19th century, a machine was invented to make metal nibs, which solved the sharpening problem—not the quill pen itself.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **c) ideas** — The sentence states that man considered 'his ideas' so important that he tried to find ways to record 'them' (his ideas) on paper.

2. **b) write finely on paper** — The stylus wrote on stone or wood, whereas when paper was introduced, a much finer instrument was needed—a problem solved by the quill pen.

3. **d) keep the end of the quill sharp** — Paragraph 4 explains that because the quill had to be sharpened often, the penknife was made for this purpose.

4. **c) it didn't need to be sharpened** — Paragraph 5 states that inventing a machine to make a nib out of metal 'solved the problem of sharpening'.

5. **d) the ink was put into the holder** — Paragraph 5 says that in 1884, Lewis Waterman found a way of putting the ink into the holder, and the fountain pen was complete.

6. **b) With a pen made from bamboo, man could use ink.** — Paragraph 3 explains that Egyptians developed pen and ink using bamboo stems filled with ink. The stylus marked stone or wood (not paper), the quill was used from the 6th century (not fountain pen), and Egyptians made bamboo pens (not quills).


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **B) How writing tools evolved over time to meet new needs** — The passage traces writing instruments in chronological order from the ancient stylus and Egyptian bamboo pen to the quill, fountain pen, and ball-point pen.

2. **C) New writing tools were created to overcome the limits of older ones.** — Each major invention addressed a limitation of the previous tool—finer writing on paper led to the quill, frequent sharpening led to the metal nib, and dipping into an ink bottle led to the fountain pen.

3. **A) useful and effective** — In 'make marks on stone or wood' and 'was very practical', 'practical' means useful and effective for everyday needs.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Tracing the Evolution of the Pen):**

Over thousands of years, humans created progressively better writing tools to record their ideas. People began with the stylus to mark stone and wood, and the Egyptians later used ink-filled bamboo stems. When paper arrived, the finer quill pen replaced bamboo, though it needed frequent sharpening with a penknife. In the 19th century, machine-made metal nibs ended the need for sharpening. Finally, Lewis Waterman placed ink inside the holder to complete the fountain pen in 1884, followed by Laszlo Biro's even more popular ball-point pen in 1943.


**Task 2 (The Value of Handwriting in a Digital Age):**

Even though digital devices dominate modern communication, I believe handwriting with a pen remains an important skill that should not disappear. First, writing by hand helps people remember information better and organize their thoughts more carefully than typing quickly on a keyboard. For example, students who take handwritten notes often understand complex lessons more deeply because they must summarize key ideas in their own words. Second, a pen requires no battery, internet connection, or electricity, making it reliable in almost any situation—from filling out official forms to jotting down quick reminders. Finally, handwritten letters and cards carry a personal touch that digital messages cannot easily replace. Of course, computers and smartphones are much faster for editing long documents and sharing work across distances. Nevertheless, teaching and practicing handwriting alongside digital skills ensures that people stay creative, focused, and independent of technology when necessary.


---


## Passage 97: Popular vs. Quality Newspapers (`raw1_097` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **sensational headlines**

2. **double meanings**

3. **long paragraph**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) Popular newspapers are often more confusing for learners than quality ones.** — The speaker explains that EFL students mistakenly assume quality newspapers are harder, when in reality popular newspapers are often harder for them to understand.

2. **B) To make readers curious enough to read the article** — According to the passage, headlines in popular newspapers do not explain the news item; instead, they puzzle the reader so that he will be persuaded to read the article to find out the meaning.

3. **A) They assume readers lack the patience for long paragraphs.** — The passage states that editors are afraid readers will not be patient enough to read a long paragraph, so nearly every sentence becomes a paragraph in itself.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **True** — The passage states that popular newspapers are written for people who do not have much time for reading, may not read as fast, or do not enjoy reading as much as quality-newspaper readers.

2. **Not Given** — The passage compares the style and readability of popular and quality newspapers, but it never mentions their prices.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **c) they think they will learn the language better in this way** — Paragraph 1 says students naturally suppose that reading an English newspaper will be a good way of improving their knowledge of the language.

2. **a) quality newspapers are less difficult to read than popular newspapers** — Students assume quality newspapers will be even harder than popular ones, so 'the opposite is usually the case' means quality newspapers are actually easier to understand.

3. **d) All of the above are correct.** — Paragraph 2 states that popular newspapers are written for people who haven't got much time for reading, do not read as fast, or do not enjoy reading as much.

4. **b) interesting** — Editors use 'sensational headlines' to attract readers' attention and persuade them to read the news item, so 'interesting' (attention-grabbing) is the closest meaning among the options.

5. **c) the headlines** — The previous sentence refers to 'The headlines', and 'On the contrary, they are often designed to puzzle the reader' continues describing those headlines.

6. **c) the headlines** — Continuing from the previous sentence about headlines puzzling readers, 'they use double meanings' refers to the headlines (and the following sentence explicitly reintroduces 'Editors').

7. **d) The headlines are big and black.** — Long sentences, puzzling headlines that fail to explain the news, and confusing headlines are disadvantages, whereas printing headlines in big black type is simply a visual style used to attract attention rather than a comprehension problem.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **D) To explain why popular newspapers are harder for language learners than expected** — The passage explains why popular newspapers, despite seeming simpler, actually create difficulties for EFL students through puzzling headlines, double meanings, and fragmented one-sentence paragraphs.

2. **A) Quality newspapers have clearer headlines and more connected paragraphs.** — Since 'the opposite is usually the case' when learners think quality newspapers will be more difficult than popular ones, quality newspapers are likely clearer and easier for EFL students to follow.

3. **C) convinced or encouraged** — In 'so that he will be persuaded to read the item', 'persuaded' means convinced or encouraged to take action.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summarizing Challenges of Popular Newspapers):**

Many English learners assume that quality newspapers are harder to read than popular ones, yet the reverse is usually true. Popular newspapers target busy readers who may dislike long reading sessions. Consequently, editors rely on eye-catching headlines that hide the story's meaning, use wordplay with double meanings, and turn single sentences into separate paragraphs. While these techniques attract casual readers, they often confuse language learners and make popular newspapers surprisingly difficult to follow.


**Task 2 (Advice on Reading English News for Learners):**

Reading news in English is a great habit for language learners, and choosing the right kind of source makes a big difference. Based on the passage, quality newspapers are generally more useful than popular newspapers for improving English. First, quality newspapers usually write clear headlines that summarize the article, helping learners predict the main topic before reading. By contrast, puzzling headlines and double meanings in popular papers waste time and create frustration. Second, full paragraphs in quality newspapers show how ideas connect naturally through linking words, which teaches students both reading comprehension and good writing structure. However, learners should still choose topics they genuinely enjoy, such as technology, sports, or culture, and start with short articles rather than trying to read an entire newspaper at once. Using an online dictionary sparingly for key unknown words will also keep reading enjoyable. Overall, clear and well-structured news articles provide the best support for language growth.


---


## Passage 98: Acid Rain (1) (`raw1_098` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **poisonous gases**

2. **3,000 / 3000 / three thousand**

3. **ecological**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) Scandinavia and Central Europe** — The passage states that measures have been taken in Scandinavia and Central Europe to stop pollution before it is dumped on the environment.

2. **B) Reported damage to large areas of forest and lakeland** — A leading environmental group notes that damage has been reported in large areas of forest and lakeland, which is forcing politicians to act.

3. **D) A major international initiative to fight acid rain** — The final sentence states that a major international initiative to combat acid rain is expected in the near future.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **False** — A leading environmental group states that five years ago the issue was not being treated seriously.

2. **True** — The passage warns that if governments do nothing, they will face the accusation in two or three years' time that they allowed forests to die.


### Reading Cevapları

**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular:**

1. **coal or oil** — In the opening sentence, "Acid rain is caused by burning coal or oil," the phrase "either fuel" refers back to coal or oil.

2. **poisonous gases** — 'Which' is a relative pronoun referring to the immediately preceding noun phrase 'poisonous gases'.

3. **the pollution** — In this clause, 'it' refers back to 'the pollution' mentioned earlier in the same sentence.

4. **Burning coal or oil, which releases poisonous gases into the atmosphere.** — The first paragraph directly states that acid rain is caused by burning coal or oil.

5. **To convince other countries that the problem has to be considered as a major ecological threat.** — The second paragraph explains that the diplomatic campaign was launched to persuade other countries to treat acid rain as a major ecological threat.

6. **They are forced to take action because damage has been reported in large areas of forest and lakeland.** — The environmental group explains that reported damage in large areas of forest and lakeland is forcing politicians to take action.

7. **Acid rain (and the damage it causes to forests and lakeland).** — In context, "This problem must be solved quickly" refers to acid rain and its environmental damage.

8. **They will be accused of allowing the forests to die.** — The quote states that if governments do nothing, they will face the accusation that they have allowed the forests to die.

9. **A major international initiative to combat acid rain.** — The final sentence explicitly states that a major international initiative to combat acid rain is expected soon.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları:**

1. **B) The causes of acid rain and growing international efforts to combat it** — The passage covers the causes of acid rain, research and regional efforts in Europe, growing pressure on politicians due to forest and lake damage, and the expectation of an international initiative.

2. **C) Air pollution travels across borders, so international cooperation is needed** — Poisonous gases can be transported long distances in the atmosphere, so countries need each other's cooperation rather than acting alone; hence the diplomatic campaign.

3. **A) address and deal with** — In "a decision to tackle the problem has been taken", 'tackle' means to deal with or address a difficult issue.


### Writing Model Cevapları

**Task 1 (Summary: Causes and Responses to Acid Rain):**

Acid rain results from burning coal or oil, which sends toxic gases high into the atmosphere and across borders. In response, most Western European countries have decided to address the issue through thousands of research projects and pollution controls in Scandinavia and Central Europe. Because forests and lakes are now visibly damaged, environmental groups are pressuring governments to act quickly before further ecological loss occurs.


**Task 2 (Opinion Paragraph: International Action on Pollution):**

Air pollution does not respect national borders, so fighting acid rain requires strong international cooperation. Even if one country reduces emissions from its factories and power plants, poisonous gases from neighbouring nations can still travel through the atmosphere and damage its forests and lakes. Therefore, governments must share scientific research, agree on strict emission limits, and support cleaner energy technologies together. Without joint action, individual efforts will only have limited success, and vital ecosystems will continue to suffer.


---


## Passage 99: Acid Rain (2) (`raw1_099` · `B1`)

### Listening Cevapları

**A. Gap-Fill:**

1. **coal**

2. **drinking water**

3. **equipment**


**B. Listening MCQ:**

1. **C) It increases the amount of acid in the water** — The passage mentions that smoke from power plants contains acidic substances that fall in rain or snow, raising acid levels in lakes and rivers and killing fish.

2. **A) Economic costs** — According to the majority of environmentalists, economics is the real reason governments avoid passing anti-pollution laws.

3. **D) Canada, the northeastern United States, and Norway** — The passage states that many lakes in Canada, the northeastern United States, and Norway are already contaminated by power plant pollution.


**C. True / False / Not Given:**

1. **False** — Governments claim the causes are still not completely clear, whereas environmentalists believe the real obstacle is economics.
